--[p205]-- 7.3.2 Identifying fundamental performance limitations: a minimal-spillover approach
7.3.2.1 Minimal spillover controller: The number and the placement of the sensors and actuators with respect to the performance variables and the disturbance inputs crucially determine the achievable inferential control performance. In this section, the concept of the zero-spillover controller in (7.12) is extended towards an approach for identifying the performance limitations related to non-collocation.
--[p205]-- In the Frobenius norm, the MSC admits a closed-form solution.
Theorem 7.3.4. The non-parametric controller that minimizes $\Vert T _ { e _ { z } } ( \omega ) \Vert _ { F }$ in (7.15) at frequency ω is the minimal-spillover controller,
$$
--[p206]-- \begin{array} { r l } { \mathrm { r a n k } ( T _ { \mathrm { s e n s } } ^ { \mathrm { m s } } ) } & { = \operatorname* { m i n } ( n _ { z } , n _ { d } - n _ { y } ) } \\ { \mathrm { r a n k } ( T _ { \mathrm { a c t } } ^ { \mathrm { m s } } ) } & { = \operatorname* { m i n } ( n _ { d } , n _ { z } - n _ { u } ) } \end{array} \ .\tag{7.23}
$$
The proof is presented in Section 7.A.2.
--[p206]-- The term $T _ { \mathrm { s e n s } } ^ { \mathrm { m s } }$ represents the error contribution due to a potentially insuficient number of sensors $n _ { y }$ to estimate the $n _ { d }$ disturbances. The dual term $T _ { \mathrm { a c t } } ^ { \mathrm { m s } }$ represents the contribution due to a potentially insuficient number of actuators $n _ { u }$ to control the $n _ { z }$ performance variables.

===== [p195-197] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.1 Introduction (21块) =====
--[p196]-- Although the inferential control problem for multivariable systems is relevant in industrial precision motion systems, at present design methods that i) can deal with multiple non-collocated disturbances in addition to unmeasured performance variables, and ii) address robustness explicitly and non-conservatively, are not available.
--[p195]-- Second, and particularly in multivariable problems, the achievable inferential performance is limited by the capability of estimating and suppressing disturbances, which in turn depends on the number and placement of sensors and actuators, compared to that of the disturbances and performance variables, respectively (Dirkx et al., 2021b).
--[p195]-- 7.1 Introduction
Structural mechanical deformations are a hampering factor in achieving the everincreasing performance demands in high-precision positioning systems. An example of such system is the wafer stage, used in the production of integrated circuits (Heertjes et al., 2020). To meet the often nanometer-tight performance requirements, traditional control techniques that assume the stage dynamics to be represented as a rigid body (Van de Wal et al., 2002) are no longer adequate.
--[p195]-- The explicit distinction between measured variables and performance variables, and between disturbance inputs and control inputs, has important implications for the control problem. First, the inferential control performance relies heavily on inevitably uncertain models, hence addressing robustness is especially crucial in inferential control problems (Oomen et al., 2014).
--[p196]-- (2014), to inferential control problems with non-collocated inputs.
This chapter is organized as follows. In Section 7.2, the wafer stage setup is introduced and the control problem is formulated. The best achievable inferential control performance is analyzed in Section 7.3. In Section 7.4, the $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ optimal inferential control design framework for multivariable systems is established.
--[p196]-- This framework extends upon the optimal robust control methods for the traditional control problem as presented in Sebastian and Salapaka (2005); Steinbuch and Norg (1998); Van de Wal et al. (2002) by including unmeasurable performance variables, but non-collocated disturbance inputs cannot be handled.
In related work Dirkx et al.
--[p196]-- Since inferential control performance objectives are not addressed, these techniques do not necessarily lead to high performance in terms of the unmeasured point of interest.
A $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ optimal control design approach that explicitly addresses robust inferential control performance requirements is presented in Oomen et al. (2014).
--[p197]-- Notations: The pseudo-inverse of a matrix $\ b X \in \mathbb { C } ^ { m \times n }$ is defined as $X ^ { \dagger } =$ $X ^ { H } ( X X ^ { H } ) ^ { - 1 }$ when $m \leq n$ , and $X ^ { \dag } = ( X ^ { H } X ) ^ { - 1 } X ^ { H }$ when $m \geq n$ . Operation $\sigma ( X )$ yields the singular values of X, and $\bar { \sigma } ( X )$ the largest singular value. Let $\mathcal { R H } _ { \infty }$ denote the space of proper real-rational stable transfer function matrices (Zhou et al., 1996, p.
--[p197]-- The pair $\{ N , D \}$ is a right coprime factorization (rcf) of P if ${ P = N D ^ { - 1 } }$ with $N , D \in \mathcal { R } \mathcal { H } _ { \infty }$ , and $\exists X _ { r } , Y _ { r } \in \mathcal { R H } _ { \infty }$ such that $X _ { r } D + Y _ { r } N = I$ . Left coprime factorizations (lcf) are obtained by dual definitions.
--[p196]-- The aim of this chapter is to improve the performance of such systems by mitigating the control limitations through a multivariable robust inferential control approach.
The main contributions in this chapter are:
1. A framework for the analysis of performance limitations induced by noncollocation in multivariable inferential control problems.
2. A $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ optimal inferential control design framework for noncollocated systems.
3.
--[p196]-- (2021b), a $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ optimal inferential control design framework is presented for systems with non-collocated disturbances. The multivariable problem setting and the associated limitations are not addressed and hence the techniques are not generally compatible to this class of problems. In prior work Dirkx and Oomen (2021), multivariable aspects are addressed but only in the context of nominal control.
--[p196]-- The estimation and suppression of disturbances using disturbance-observer based control (Chen et al., 2015) is well-developed for problems wherein the performance variables are directly measured and the disturbances are collocated with the inputs, e.g., Han (2009); Schrijver and Van Dijk (2002). Observer-based rejection of non-collocated disturbances is reported in, e.g., Sariyildiz et al. (2019), for single input systems.
--[p195]-- Control techniques are required that explicitly address the flexible dynamics to counteract the efect of structural deformations (Van Herpen et al., 2014).
Accounting for structural deformations significantly increases the complexity of the control design. This applies particularly to systems where the performance variables, also referred to as the point of interest, cannot directly be measured (Oomen et al., 2014).
--[p195]-- Third, achieving optimal disturbance estimation and suppression within these limitations requires a particularly shaped multivariable controller, which imposes specific demands on the control design methods. These aspects are only partly addressed in existing approaches.

--[p197]-- Section 7.5 presents a method for the construction of model sets for high-performance robust inferential control. In Section 7.6, simulation results are presented that illustrate the limitations due to non-collocation. --[p195]-- For example, in wafer stages the performance is required at the point-of-exposure, while the sensors are located at diferent locations in the stage (Butler, 2011). In printing systems, performance is required at the printing interface, while the sensor is typically mounted on the motor, with flexible dynamics in between (Winarto et al., 2015).
--[p196]-- Contribution 2 extends the classical four-block $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ design problem (Van de Wal et al., 2002) to a twelve-block inferential problem, including a multivariable and directional weighting filter design approach. Contribution 3 extends the dual-Youla-Kuˇcera uncertainty structure (Anderson, 1998), and the theory on non-normalized coprime factorizations in Lanzon and Papageorgiou (2009); Oomen and Bosgra (2012); Oomen et al.
--[p195]-- When the performance variables cannot be measured, they must be estimated (inferred) from the measurements using a model that represents the dynamics between the measured and performance variables (Parrish and Brosilow, 1985).
Additional complexity arises when the disturbances are not collocated with the control inputs. Examples include the disturbances induced by rollers in printing systems (Winarto et al., 2015), or the immersion hood in wafer scanners (Butler, 2011).
--[p196]-- An approach for constructing model sets for achieving high-performance robust inferential control for non-collocated systems.

Contribution 1 extends the concept of zero-spillover control (Hong and Bernstein, 1998) to a performance analysis framework based on minimal-spillover control.
--[p195]-- Especially, the presence of spatially distributed disturbances often leads to highly complex deformations. In the presence of these so-called non-collocated disturbances, a model of the dynamics between the disturbance inputs and the control inputs must be incorporated explicitly in the control design (Francis and Wonham, 1976; Hong and Bernstein, 1998), which introduces further complexity in the inferential control problem.

--[p197]-- 7.2.1 Wafer stage positioning
7.2.1.1 Wafer stage setup: Wafer scanners are state-of-the-art mechatronic systems that play an essential role in the production of integrated circuits (IC) (Butler, 2011). These ICs are produced through a photolithographic process, in which a photoresist on a silicon disk, called the wafer, is exposed to a short wavelength light source. The exposure results in the imprint of the desired IC pattern into the wafer.
--[p197]-- This is illustrated in the identified non-parametric Frequency Response Function (FRF) model $\hat { P } ^ { \mathrm { F R F } }$ in Figure 7.2. The model shows predominant rigid-body behavior up to the first resonance around 120Hz. Beyond 120Hz, the dynamics is characterized by complex dynamics resulting from lightly damped resonance and antiresonance phenomena induced by internal flexibilities.

--[p125]-- 5.4.2 Wavelet-based LPM for handling missing data
In this section, the wavelet-based LPM estimator is formulated for FRF identification in the presence of missing samples, as part of contribution 1 of this chapter. The extension upon the traditional LPM (5.20) is twofold. First, the local criteria (5.20) are combined into a single global criterion to facilitate handling the global efect of the missing samples.

===== [p90-91] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.6.3 Results (7块) =====
--[p90]-- Schroeder phase selection (Schroeder, 1970) is fast but leads to a constraint excess of a factor $> 2 . 5$ . The time-frequency domain swapping algorithm in Van der Ouderaa et al. (1988b) is originally a SISO method, and is applied to the worst-case signal per iteration. This method fails to converge in the multivariable setting, since the optimization of a single signal $z _ { p }$ deteriorates the other signals.
--[p91]-- The MIMO approach delivers signals that tightly satisfy the $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constraints for each of the elements $p , e .$ A similar observation is made for the other signals in $z$ (not depicted). Thus, in this approach, the available design freedom is well-exploited, which is beneficial for achieving a good signal-to-noise ratio. This is in sharp contrast to the SIMO approach, by which the constraints are only tight in the diagonal elements.
--[p90]-- This demonstrates the capability of the smoothing-based algorithm for multivariable $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constrained signal design, as well as its high computational eficiency.
3
3.6.3.2 Computed signals: The output signals y computed by Algorithm 3.3 for the SIMO and MIMO designs are shown in Figure 3.8.
--[p90]-- The $L _ { p } { \mathrm { - m e t h o d } }$ (Guillaume et al., 1991), using sequence $p = \{ 2 ^ { 2 } , \ 2 ^ { 3 } , \ldots , 2 ^ { 9 } \}$ , converges in ∼40 iterations, but the computation time of ∼500s is substantial. The smoothing method refers to Algorithm 3.3 with PRCG solver, and gradient computation by Theorem 3.4.1. This method shows convergence in ∼150 iterations, and is ∼50 times faster than the $L _ { p }$ method.
--[p91]-- 3.6.3.3 Identified FRF model: A $2 \times 2$ selection of the entries of the identified FRF models is shown in Figure 3.9. A significant reduction of the uncertainty in observed when using the MIMO design, especially below 120Hz. The cost value $\mathcal { T }$ is a factor 11 smaller for the MIMO design than for the SIMO design.
--[p91]-- These results verify the efectiveness of the two-stage multivariable OED framework and demonstrate that the developed techniques enable a quality improvement of identified non-parametric FRF models.

===== [p38-40] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.2.3 Optimal experiment design objective (17块) =====
--[p39]-- This aspect plays a crucial role throughout this chapter.
2.2.3.3 OED problem in explicit form: By combining the cost function (2.7) with the expression for element-wise powers (2.10), the general design problem (2.6) is formulated explicitly as
$$
--[p38]-- \begin{array} { l l } { \underset { W } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e } } } & { \mathcal { I } \left( W \right) } \\ { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o } } & { g ( W ) \leq 0 . } \end{array}\tag{2.6}
$$
The criterion $\mathcal { I } \left( W \right)$ and the inequality constraints $g ( W )$ are specified in the forthcoming subsections.
2.2.3.1 Cost function: The cost function in (2.6) is chosen as the weighted A-optimality criterion
$$
--[p39]-- \mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } | \Xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) | ^ { 2 } ,\tag{2.9}
$$
where $\Xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] }$ is the $D F T o f \xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] }$
Let the signal vector $\xi ^ { [ e ] }$ be a function of the excitation signal $w ^ { [ e ] }$ through $\xi ^ { [ e ] } = G _ { \xi } w ^ { [ e ] }$ . Then, the element-wise power $\mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] }$ is expressed as
$$
--[p39]-- where $H _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } ) \triangleq G _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) G _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } )$ , ∀k and $G _ { \xi _ { i } }$ denotes the i-th row of $G _ { \xi }$ System $G _ { \xi }$ depends on the selected signals in $\xi ^ { [ e ] }$ .
--[p40]-- 2.2.3.4 Two-step design procedure: Solving the optimal experiment design problem (NLP) requires prior system knowledge in the form of the problem data $\gamma , S , H _ { \xi }$ . This data can be acquired from preliminary identification experiments, see $\mathrm { e . g . }$ , Dirkx et al. (2019); Hildebrand and Gevers (2002). Likewise, a two-step design approach is considered in this chapter:
1) preliminary experiments for prior data acquisition,
2) optimal experiments to produce high quality FRFs.
--[p39]-- \mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } | \Xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) | ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \langle H _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } ) , W ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) W ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) \rangle ,\tag{2.10}
$$
--[p39]-- For many mechatronic systems, however, the total power does not necessarily represent a relevant or even meaningful physical quantity. Particularly for motion systems, like the wafer stage in Section 2.2.1, the physically relevant powers are typically related to individual elements $\xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] } ( t )$ , $\mathrm { e . g . }$ , representing an individual actuator voltage or current.
--[p38]-- \mathcal { I } \left( W \right) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \mathrm { T r } \left( M ( k ) \mathcal { C } _ { \hat { G } } ( k ) \right) .\tag{2.7}
$$
Here, $M ( k ) \in \mathbb { R } _ { + } ^ { n _ { y } n _ { u } \times n _ { y } n _ { u } }$ is a user-defined weighting matrix that can be designed towards the intended use of the FRF model, e.g., to connect the identification criterion to a robust closed-loop control objective (Oomen et al., 2013).
--[p39]-- Such constraints are directly relevant in typical mechatronic systems, e.g., due to actuator limitations. Moreover, a power-constrained design may serve as an intermediate design step towards a peak amplitude-constrained excitation design (Manchester, 2009).
In traditional OED for MIMO systems, constraints on the total power are considered, e.g., in Barenthin et al. (2008); Mehra (1974b), which represents the power in all signals over all experiments.
--[p40]-- \begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \underset { W ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \in \mathbb { C } ^ { n _ { u } } } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e } } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \gamma ( k ) \mathrm { T r } \left( S ( \Omega _ { k } ) \sum _ { e = 1 } ^ { n _ { u } } W ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) W ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) S ^ { H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) \right) ^ { - 1 } } \\ & { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o } \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \langle H _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } ) , W ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) W ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) \rangle \leq c _ { \xi _ { i } } , \quad \forall i , e , } \end{array}\tag{NLP}
$$
where $\gamma ( k ) \triangleq \operatorname { T r } \left( { \mathcal { C } } _ { Y } ( k ) \right)$ and $c _ { \xi }$ contains the user-defined power limits.
--[p38]-- 2.2.3 Optimal experiment design objective
The experiment design objective in this chapter is to compute the excitations $W = \{ W ( 1 ) , \ldots , W ( N ) \}$ that minimize a scalar cost function $\mathcal { I } ( W )$ that expresses, in some sense, the accuracy of the estimate $\hat { G }$ in (2.4), while satisfying prespecified constraints on the signals w, u, and/or $y .$ This OED problem is naturally posed as the constrained optimization problem,
$$
--[p39]-- For example, $\xi ^ { [ e ] }$ could be composed as $\hat { \xi ^ { [ e ] } } = [ w ^ { [ e ] T } ~ { u ^ { [ e ] T } } ~ y ^ { [ e ] T } ] ^ { T }$ such that $G _ { \xi } = [ I ~ S ^ { T } ~ { \bar { G } } S ^ { T } ] ^ { T }$
The use of constraints on the element-wise powers (2.9) instead of on the total power (2.8) complicates the design problem since it typically results in a nonconvex optimization problem, as will be shown in the following.
--[p40]-- Moreover, since the number of scalar decision variables is given by $N _ { d } = N ( n _ { u } ^ { 2 } - n _ { u } )$ , the problem dimension tends to become very large, particularly for systems with a large number of inputs $n _ { u }$
Solving the non-convex and potentially large-scale problem (NLP) exactly or with a guaranteed good performance is non-straightforward. This chapter presents methods to solve the multivariable OED problem.
--[p38]-- In the remainder of this chapter, without loss of generality, an open-loop criterion $M ( k ) = I $ , ∀k is assumed and hence it will be omitted in the notations. For this choice of $M ( k )$ , the cost function (2.7) represents the total variance over all frequencies and entries of $\hat { G }$ and hence constitutes a representative measure of the overall quality of the identified FRF.
2.2.3.2 Element-wise power constraints: Signal power constraints are considered.
--[p39]-- Therefore, this chapter considers element-wise power constraints instead, wherein the power in each of the signals and each of the experiments are explicitly distinguished.
Definition 2.2.1. The element-wise power of a scalar-valued signal $\xi _ { i } ^ { [ e ] } ( t ) , i \in$ $1 , \ldots , n _ { \xi }$ out of a vector-valued signal $\xi ^ { [ e ] } ( t ) = [ \xi _ { 1 } ^ { [ e ] T } ( t ) , \dots , \xi _ { n _ { \xi } } ^ { [ e ] T } ( t ) ] ^ { T }$ is given by
$$
--[p39]-- Applying Parseval’s power theorem, the total power of the set of vector-valued signals, say $\xi ^ { [ e ] } ( t ) \in \bar { \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \xi } \times N } }$ 2 $e = 1 , \ldots , n _ { u }$ , is expressed as the scalar quantity
$$
\mathcal { P } _ { \xi } ^ { \mathrm { t o t } } = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \mathrm { T r } \left( \sum _ { e = 1 } ^ { n _ { u } } \Xi ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \Xi ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) \right) ,\tag{2.8}
$$
where $\Xi ^ { [ e ] }$ is the DFT of $\xi ^ { [ e ] }$ .
--[p40]-- The constraints are scaled such that max $c _ { \xi _ { i } } = 1$ , without loss of generality.
Program (NLP) constitutes the minimization of an inverse quadratic form over an intersection of ellipsoids. This problem is non-convex and NP-hard in general (Nesterov, 1998).

===== [p200-202] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.2.2 Robust inferential control problem (10块) =====
--[p202]-- By virtue of (7.5), the following performance is guaranteed when the robust controller $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } }$ is implemented on the true system
$$
\mathcal { I } ( P _ { o } , C ^ { \mathrm { o p t } } ) \leq \mathcal { I } ( P _ { o } , C ^ { \mathrm { R P } } ) \leq \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( \mathcal { P } , C ^ { \mathrm { R P } } ) .\tag{7.10}
$$
--[p201]-- To guarantee performance of the model-based controller when implemented on the true system $P _ { o } ,$ a model set $\mathcal { P }$ is considered that encompasses $P _ { o }$
$$
P _ { o } \in \mathcal { P } ( \hat { P } , \Delta ) ,\tag{7.5}
$$
where $\Delta$ represents model uncertainty (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005).
--[p202]-- The presence of non-collocated performance variables and non-collocated disturbances has important implications for the controller structure, its dynamics, and for the performance that can be achieved. This imposes the following requirements for the components $C , { \mathcal { I } } _ { \mathrm { W C } }$ , and $\mathcal { P }$ in (7.9):
--[p201]-- The performance variable $z _ { p }$ is not measured during operation and hence must be inferred from the measurements $y _ { p }$ using a model $\hat { P }$ that reflects the true system $P _ { o }$ . Since any model is an approximation of reality, addressing model uncertainty in the controller design is particularly important in inferential control.
--[p201]-- To comply with standard robust control design approaches (Zhou et al., 1996), the set $\mathcal { P }$ is constructed by an uncertainty $\Delta \in \mathcal { R } \mathcal { H } _ { \infty }$ around a nominal model $\hat { P }$ by the LFT interconnection,
$$
\mathcal { P } = \left\{ P : P = \mathcal { F } _ { u } ( \hat { Q } , \Delta ) , \ \Delta \in \Delta \right\} ,\tag{7.6}
$$
--[p202]-- Depending on the selection of $\hat { Q } ,$ the LFT interconnection (7.6) encompasses diferent common uncertainty structures such as (inverse) additive, multiplicative, or coprime factor-based structures (Zhou et al., 1996).
The worst-case control criterion with respect to the model set $\mathcal { P }$ is formulated as
$$
{ \mathcal { T } } _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( { \mathcal { P } } , C ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { P \in { \mathcal { P } } } { \mathcal { I } } ( P , C ) .\tag{7.8}
$$
--[p202]-- The optimal robust inferential controller is expressed in terms of the worst-case model in the set.
Definition 7.2.5 (Optimal robust inferential controller). Given the model set $\mathcal { P } ( \hat { P } , \Delta )$ , the optimal robust inferential controller is
$$
C ^ { \mathrm { R P } } = \underset { C } { \arg \operatorname* { m i n } } \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( \mathcal { P } , C ) .\tag{7.9}
$$
--[p200]-- 7.2.2 Robust inferential control problem
The inferential control problem consists in the minimization of the inferential control error $e _ { z } = r _ { z } - z _ { p } ,$ with $z _ { p }$ in (7.1) and $r _ { z }$ a given reference signal, by appropriate design of a feedback controller
$$
\begin{array} { r } { u _ { p } = C ( r _ { z } , y _ { p } ) . } \end{array}\tag{7.3}
$$
Optimal controller design is considered.
7
Definition 7.2.4 (Optimal inferential controller).
--[p201]-- where $\hat { Q }$ contains the model $\hat { P } .$ , such that $\hat { P } = \mathcal { F } _ { u } ( \hat { Q } , 0 )$ , and $\pmb { \Delta }$ is an unstructured norm-bounded subset
$$
\Delta = \left\{ \Delta \in \mathcal { R H } _ { \infty } : \left\| \Delta \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \gamma \right\} .\tag{7.7}
$$
7
--[p201]-- Given the true system $P _ { o }$ and the interconnection (7.1), (7.3), the optimal inferential controller is
$$
C ^ { \mathrm { o p t } } = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { C } \mathcal { I } ( P _ { o } , C ) ,\tag{7.4}
$$
where $\mathcal { I }$ is the objective function.
The specific objective function $\mathcal { I }$ will be selected later.

$$
b ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \triangleq \langle \mathcal { W } ^ { [ e ] \langle j \rangle } ( k ) , \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] \langle j \rangle } ( k ) \rangle = \sum _ { i = 2 } ^ { n _ { u } } \lambda _ { i } \left( \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] \langle j \rangle } ( k ) \right) .

which shows that candidate solution $X ^ { [ e ] \left. j \right. } ( k )$ satisfies the third constraint in (SSDR). Hence, $X ^ { [ e ] \left. j \right. } ( k )$ is a feasible solution at iteration $j + 1$ if iteration $j = 1$ is feasible.
--[p61]-- 2.A.3 Proof of Lemma 2.4.6
Lemma 2.A.1 (Theorem 2.1 in (Rao, 1979)). Consider a matrix $A \in \mathbb { H } ^ { n }$ with eigenvalues in descending order, $i . e . , \lambda _ { 1 } ( A ) > \lambda _ { 2 } ( A ) > . . . > \lambda _ { n } ( A )$ . Then, for any semi-orthogonal $B \in \{ B | \ B \in \mathbb { C } ^ { n \times m } , B ^ { H } B = I _ { m } \}$ it holds that

===== [p113-114] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.1 Introduction (15块) =====
--[p113]-- The estimation of additional variables has a negative impact on the locality of the estimation problem, leading to a performance deterioration for an increasing number of data gaps.
Other approaches aim at reconstructing the missing samples. In Stoica et al. (2009), a non-parametric iterative spectrum reconstruction scheme is presented. In Broersen et al. (2004), reconstruction is based on a statistical approach. Neither of the approaches are applied for FRF identification. In Ugryumova et al.
--[p113]-- 5.1 Introduction
Accurate non-parametric Frequency Response Function (FRF) identification is a key step towards high-performance control of complex systems. FRF identification, as opposed to first principles modeling, is considered a fast and inexpensive approach to obtain thorough insight into the system dynamics, while requiring only mild assumptions on the actual system (Ljung et al., 1987).
--[p114]-- Key principles of wavelet-based convolution and the time-frequency plane are discussed in view of the missing samples problem in Section 5.3. In Section 5.4, the wavelet-based identification framework is presented that extends the existing frequency-domain LPM to a time-frequency plane approach. Herein, the TIE and TVE are presented as specializations within the general framework, and their bias and variance properties are analyzed.
--[p114]-- (2014); Ugryumova et al. (2015), the LPM is extended to estimate frequencydomain system parameters along with the missing samples. Due to the global nature of the time-frequency domain transform, errors in the estimates of the missing samples induce errors in the frequency-domain system parameters at all frequencies. This efect is amplified for an increasing number of missing samples due to an increasing correlation between the estimated parameters.
--[p114]-- Another line of research has focused on the development of time-dependent frequency analysis techniques for transient signals. In particular, the short-time Fourier transform (Cvetkovic, 2000) and the wavelet transform (Daubechies, 1990; Daubechies, 1992) are widely used in, e.g., signal processing (Jansen et al., 1997), finite-element methods (Chen et al., 2004), and image compression (Simard and Malvar, 2001), but have not been applied for FRF identification from incomplete data.
--[p113]-- This is exploited in the Local Polynomial Method (LPM) (Schoukens et al., 2009), where the transient is approximated by a polynomial function in a local frequency-domain window. Extensions to local rational approximation functions are developed in the Local Rational Method (LRM) (McKelvey and Gu´erin, 2012). The LPM has shown to be particularly efective for the identification of slow dynamic systems that exhibit significant transient behavior (Evers et al., 2021).
--[p113]-- In many practical applications, and especially for slow dynamic systems that require long measurement time, measurements cannot be performed without interruptions, which leads to missing samples in the data record. Missing samples may also occur due to communication link or sensor failure (Kar and Moura, 2009).
--[p113]-- (2012), a signal reconstruction approach is presented wherein an additional transient is estimated for each data gap, but application to FRF estimation is not considered. In Schoukens et al. (2012), the classical LPM is extended to locally estimate the FRF parameters together with additional transient parameters for each data gap.
--[p113]-- The mitigation of leakage errors, arising from the time-frequency domain transformation of finite-length data records, is an important aspect in non-parametric identification. As recognized in, e.g., Douce and Balmer (1985); Schoukens et al. (1997), leakage errors originate from a transient phenomenon, which has smooth frequency characteristics that depend on the system dynamics.
--[p114]-- In Section 5.5, the TIE is evaluated in simulation on a multiple inputs multiple outputs (MIMO) system. Experimental results are presented in Section 5.6, and conclusions are drawn in Section 5.7.
5
--[p113]-- Locally missing samples in the time-domain introduce global and nonsmooth efects in the frequency domain (Ugryumova et al., 2014), which complicates the use of standard identification algorithms, including the LPM.
Several techniques have been developed to deal with the missing samples in non-parametric identification. In Barb´e et al.
--[p114]-- Although important developments have been made in FRF identification from incomplete data, current approaches involve the estimation of an increasing number of auxiliary variables for an increasing amount of missing data. The aim of this chapter is to present a non-parametric FRF identification framework for periodically excited systems that avoids the estimation of additional variables and the associated drawbacks.
The main contributions of this chapter are:
1.
--[p114]-- A generalized LPM framework for the estimation of FRF models and their variance from incomplete data, that avoids the estimation of additional variables by separating the efect of the missing samples from the system characteristics. The framework encompasses:
a) a time-invariant wavelet-based estimator (TIE),
b) a time-varying wavelet-based estimator (TVE),
2. an analysis of bias and variance errors for both estimators,
3.
--[p114]-- a performance evaluation based on simulation results of a mechanical system and experimental results from a thermo-dynamic system.
Preliminary results related to contribution 1a) are presented in Dirkx et al. (2022b). Prior work on contribution 2 is presented in Dirkx et al. (2023b). In this chapter, the techniques are further developed towards a generalized framework.
This chapter is organized as follows. In Section 5.2, the problem is formulated.
--[p113]-- FRFs are used in many application domains, including flexible mechanical systems (Geerardyn et al., 2015) and thermal systems (Monteyne et al., 2013), with purposes ranging from controller synthesis (Karimi and Kammer, 2017), diagnostics (Van der Maas et al., 2016), and parametric identification (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012).

===== [p161-162] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.2.2 Model sets delivered by classical identification (8块) =====
--[p161]-- Classical prediction error (PE) identification delivers such region under the following assumptions. First, the model set $\mathcal { M }$ is suficiently complex to represent the true system, where
$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \mathcal { M } : } & { { } y ( t ) = \hat { P } ( \boldsymbol { q } , \boldsymbol { \theta } ) u ( t ) + \hat { H } ( \boldsymbol { q } , \boldsymbol { \theta } ) e ( t ) , } \end{array}
$$
--[p161]-- 6.2.2 Model sets delivered by classical identification
Achieving high-performance control demands that system identification delivers a model set that is small in view of the control criterion (6.3), yet is suficiently large to contain $P ^ { o }$ . The latter requirement gives rise to the formulation of an uncertainty region that contains $P ^ { o }$ and which is the set of systems that is compatible with the measured data and a priori assumptions.
--[p161]-- Second, the model uncertainty is defined completely by noise-induced variance errors in the model parameters θ. The region delivered by PE identification is of the following form.

===== [p43-43] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.4 Solutions to the multivariable OED problem (2块) =====
--[p43]-- 2.4 Solutions to the multivariable OED problem
This section presents methods to compute multivariable excitations. Since the OED problem (NLP) is non-convex and generally NP-hard, the main focus is on solving the problem approximately.
In Section 2.4.1, (NLP) is reformulated into a rank-one constrained Semi-Definite Program (SDP), which forms an intermediate yet instrumental step in solving the problem. In Section 2.4.2, exact solutions to two special cases are given.
--[p43]-- Section 2.4.3 presents a Sequential Semi-Definite Relaxation (SSDR) algorithm, wherein the non-convex problem is approximately solved by a sequence of convex programs. In Section 2.4.4, a Relaxation and Randomization (RR) algorithm is presented that combines a convex relaxation with a subsequent randomization step. The organization of this section is schematically depicted in Figure 2.6.

===== [p142-142] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.5.2 Identification results (2块) =====
--[p142]-- 5.5.2 Identification results
The identified FRF models and their estimated 95% confidence region bounds are shown in Figure 5.11, together with the true system ( ). The classical LPM ( ) delivers a poor model. The estimates from the TIE accurately represent the true dynamics.
--[p142]-- The uncertainty bound increases for increasing values of $p .$ This is supported by Figure 5.12, which shows a decomposition of the estimation error into bias and variance contributions for diferent values of $p ,$ see Figure 5.12. Clearly, bias is reduced for increasing values of $p .$ Concludingly, the observations are in line with the statements in Section 5.4.3 and demonstrate good performance of the presented techniques.

===== [p2-7] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance (16块) =====
--[p7]-- Fourth, a robust controller design framework is developed for systems where the performance variables cannot be measured and the disturbance locations are not collocated with the actuators, which is typical for the considered class of complex systems. Robust control criteria and non-conservative uncertainty structures are presented that can handle the non-collocated inferential control problem.
--[p2]-- Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance
Nic Dirkx
--[p6]-- The productivity and product quality achieved by wafer scanners are determined by the positioning performance of mechatronic systems, which are required to operate at extreme speeds while maintaining sub-nanometer precision. Automatic control is the essential technology that enables achieving these requirements. To meet the increasingly stringent performance requirements for future systems, lightweight flexible mechanical designs in conjunction with advanced control strategies are envisaged.
--[p7]-- Third, a framework is presented that enables high-performance robust control by exploiting the potential of non-normalized coprime factors to establish tight connections between the criteria for experiment design, system identification, and robust control. By reducing conservatism throughout the design procedure, the techniques lead to an improved control performance compared to traditional approaches, which is confirmed by results from a complex motion stage.
--[p6]-- Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance
Technological innovations play an increasingly important role in modern life. In particular, the emergence of the internet of things, referring to the ongoing trend of globally interconnecting people and electronic devices, puts smart technology more and more in a central position in society.
The need for smart technological solutions comes with an ever-higher demand for computing power.
--[p6]-- This demand is continuously pushing semiconductor industry to deliver more powerful and more afordable computer chips, presenting it with enormous technical and scientific challenges. These challenges are particularly encountered in the production of the highly sophisticated lithography machines, called wafer scanners, that are responsible for the most critical step in the chip manufacturing process.

--[p7]-- The aim of this thesis is to improve the process from experiment to data, from data to models, and from models to control design via the development of experiment design, identification, and control algorithms. This thesis includes the following contributions towards improving the performance of model-based control in high-precision mechatronics.
First, experiment design algorithms are developed for the efective identification of systems with a large number of inputs and outputs.
--[p5]-- Dit proefschrift is goedgekeurd door de promotoren en de samenstelling van de promotiecommissie is als volgt:
voorzitter: prof.dr.ir. P.D. Anderson

--[p7]-- Results from a complex wafer stage show that the techniques lead to improved performance at the unmeasured point of interest compared to classical control approaches.
The approaches presented in this thesis are successfully validated in practice on a range of systems, including wafer scanners, a vibration isolation system, opto-mechatronic equipment and thermo-dynamic devices.
--[p7]-- This is done by optimally exploiting directionality in input design within constraints that are representative of physical limitations in precision mechatronics. In addition, the availability of difuse prior knowledge is addressed by an algorithm that explicitly incorporates the associated prior uncertainty in the experiment design.
--[p7]-- The results confirm their applicability to complex mechatronics and their potential for improving the performance of future systems beyond the current state-of-art.
--[p6]-- This makes the design of experiments an essential part of system identification. Eficiently obtaining accurate models of complex lithography equipment poses challenging experiment design and identification problems. This applies especially to systems with many actuators and sensors, and with dynamics that are predominated by lightly damped resonance phenomena, as envisioned for the future mechatronic systems.
--[p6]-- In pursuing advanced control strategies to improve machine performance, the availability of models that accurately describe the physical behavior of the controlled system is of vital importance. The employment of system identification, which entails methods to build mathematical models from experimental data, is a key step to obtaining such models. The accuracy of the identified model is crucially determined by the conditions under which the identification experiments are performed.
--[p7]-- The technique uses a wavelet-based transformation to remove the efects of missing samples or possible mishaps in the measured data from the computed system’s frequency response. Hereby, the approach enables preserving time-eficiency of the identification experiments, which is particularly relevant for systems that require long or many identification experiments. This is confirmed by experimental results from a thermo-mechanical device.
--[p7]-- Validation on complex lithography equipment shows that the techniques lead to both a significant improvement in model accuracy and a reduction of experiment time and computational load compared to traditional approaches.
Second, a non-parametric identification approach is developed that enables obtaining accurate system models when measured data is only partly available.

--[p135]-- 5.4.4 Time-varying wavelet-based estimator (TVE)
The main idea of the TVE is to avoid data discardment by adjusting the wavelet, at each sample of the convolution, to the pattern of the missing samples. As such, the TVE addresses the main limitation of the TIE. In this section, the timevarying wavelet-based transformation and the resulting TVE are presented, and its bias and variance properties are analyzed. This section constitutes contributions 1b) and 2 of this chapter.

===== [p43-45] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.4.1 Rank-constrained SDP and semidefinite relaxation (10块) =====
--[p43]-- Finally, by applying the Schur-complement, this conic constraint is replaced by a Linear Matrix Inequality (LMI) constraint. As a result, (NLP) is equivalent to the rank-one constrained semi-definite program
$$
--[p43]-- Next, the nonlinear cost function is ‘linearized’ by using the standard result that minimization of $\mathrm { T r } \left( \left( S ( \Omega _ { k } ) \Phi _ { w } ( k ) S ^ { H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) \right) ^ { - 1 } \right)$ is equivalent to minimizing the trace of the auxiliary variable $Z ( k ) \in \mathbb { H } ^ { n _ { u } }$ under the conic constraint $Z ( k ) - S ^ { - H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) \Phi _ { w } ^ { - 1 } ( k ) S ^ { - 1 } ( \Omega _ { k } ) \succeq 0$ .
--[p43]-- Performing the variable substitution (2.15) in problem (NLP) leads to a nonlinear program, constituting the minimization of an inverse form over a set of linear constraints and rank constraints.
--[p44]-- ht) = 1 , \quad \quad \forall k , e . } \end{array}\tag{RSDP}
$$
Due to the non-convex and discontinuous nature of rank constraints, solving (RSDP) is as dificult as solving (NLP). However, a useful property of (RSDP) is that its fundamental dificulty is captured fully in the rank constraints. This enables formulation of the semi-definite relaxation of (RSDP), which is obtained by eliminating the rank constraints, leading to
$$
--[p44]-- \begin{array} { r l } { \underset { \displaystyle \mathfrak { G } _ { \omega } ^ { [ \omega ] } ( k ) , Z ( k ) \in \mathbb { R } _ { + } ^ { n } } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e } } } & { \quad \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \gamma ( k ) \mathrm { T r } \left( Z ( k ) \right) } \\ { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o } } & { \quad \left[ \begin{array} { l l } { Z ( k ) } & { S ^ { - H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) } \\ { S ^ { - 1 } ( \Omega _ { k } ) } & { \sum _ { e = 1 } ^ { n _ { u } } \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) } \end{array} \right] \succeq 0 , \quad \forall k } \\ & { \quad \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \langle H _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } ) , \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \rangle \leq c _ { \xi _ { i } } , } \\ & { \quad \mathrm { r a n k } \left( \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \rig
--[p43]-- \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \triangleq W ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) W ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) , \quad \mathrm { r a n k } \left( \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \right) = 1 , \quad \forall e , k ,\tag{2.15}
$$
see, e.g., Nemirovski et al. (1999); Nesterov (1998). Matrix $\Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \in \mathbb { H } _ { + } ^ { n _ { u } }$ represents the excitation spectrum associated to the e-th experiment.
--[p44]-- \begin{array} { r l } { \underset { \boldsymbol { \Phi } _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) , Z ( k ) } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e } } \quad } & { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \gamma ( k ) \mathrm { T r } \left( Z ( k ) \right) } \\ { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o } \quad } & { \displaystyle \left[ \begin{array} { l l } { Z ( k ) } & { S ^ { - H } ( \Omega _ { k } ) } \\ { S ^ { - 1 } ( \Omega _ { k } ) } & { \sum _ { e = 1 } ^ { n _ { u } } \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) } \end{array} \right] \succeq 0 , \quad \forall k } \\ & { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } \langle H _ { \xi _ { i } } ( \Omega _ { k } ) , \Phi _ { w } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \rangle \leq c _ { \xi _ { i } } , \qquad \forall i , e . } \end{array}\tag{SDR}
$$
--[p44]-- Program (SDR) is convex and can be solved eficiently, e.g., by interior-point algorithms (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004; Nesterov and Nemirovskii, 1994).
2
Even though the solution to (SDR) is generally not a solution to (RSDP), it plays an instrumental role in certifying the quality of an (approximate) solution to (RSDP).
--[p45]-- Concretely, the optimal cost value $f _ { S \mathcal { D R } } ^ { \dagger }$ of (SDR) provides a lower bound to the optimal cost value $f _ { \mathcal { R } \mathcal { S } \mathcal { D P } } ^ { * }$ of the original problem (RSDP), i.e., $f _ { S \mathcal { D R } } ^ { \dagger } \leq f _ { \mathcal { R } \mathcal { S } \mathcal { D P } } ^ { * } .$ Additionally, besides this lower bound for performance, the solution to (SDR) plays a key role in the algorithms for solving the non-convex problem (RSDP), which is presented in the following.

Example 5.3.4. An example of wavelets $\psi _ { 2 0 } ^ { [ 2 ] } , \psi _ { 2 0 } ^ { [ 3 ] }$ is shown in Figure 5.2, where $N = 5 0 0 , P = 1 , \delta = 5$ .
--[p118]-- 5.3.2 Wavelet selection
The goal of using the wavelet-based transformation in Definition 5.3.1 is to obtain a time- and frequency-domain localized representation of the data $z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ wherein the efect of the missing samples can be confined. As expressed by the Heisenberg-Gabor uncertainty principle, see, e.g., Gr¨ochenig (2001), there are fundamental limitations to the localization accuracy that can simultaneously be achieved in the two domains.
--[p119]-- The wavelets enable approximately extracting local frequency-information around the j-th frequency line within a local time-window of $\hat { l } _ { \psi } ^ { [ 2 ] } = 1 9 9 , l _ { \psi } ^ { [ 3 ] } = 2 9 8$ samples, see properties a) and c) in Definition 5.3.2. Increasing p improves the frequency-domain localization accuracy, at the cost of time-domain accuracy, see properties d) and c), respectively.
Remark 5.3.5.
--[p118]-- In particular, it is not possible to have a wavelet with finite support in the time domain that is also band limited in the frequency domain. Wavelet selection therefore involves a trade-of, and its choice is typically problem-specific.
The following class of wavelets is considered that is particularly suitable for the confinement of missing samples.
Definition 5.3.2 (Wavelet selection).
--[p119]-- The parameter $c ^ { [ p ] } = ( \delta \sqrt { P / N } ) ^ { p }$ in (5.10) is a scaling constant.
The wavelet function $\psi _ { j } ^ { \left[ p \right] }$ in Definition 5.3.2 is composed of a complex exponential multiplied by an envelope $h ^ { [ p ] }$ based on a p-th order cardinal B-spline. The p-parameter controls the smoothness of the envelope, and hereby the wavelet its time- and frequency-domain localization properties.
--[p119]-- b) $\Psi _ { j } ^ { [ p ] }$ equals zero at frequency lines $j \pm \alpha \delta P , \ \alpha , p \in \mathbb { N } _ { + }$
c) $\psi _ { j } ^ { [ p ] }$ has finite time-domain support of length $l _ { \psi } ^ { [ p ] } = p N / \delta - p + 1 , i . e .$ $\psi _ { j } ^ { \mathsf { [ } p \mathsf { ] } } [ n ] = 0 ~ f o r ~ n \geq l _ { \psi } ^ { [ p ] }$
d) $| \Psi _ { j } ^ { [ p + 1 ] } | < | \Psi _ { j } ^ { [ p ] } |$ at all but the j-th and $j \pm \alpha \delta \ – t h$ frequency lines.
--[p119]-- In the frequency domain, the DFT $\Psi _ { j } ^ { [ p ] }$ is characterized by a Dirichlet kernel, also referred to as the aliased sinc function (Levi, 1974). This gives rise to the key properties below.
Lemma 5.3.3 (Wavelet properties). The wavelet function $\psi _ { j } ^ { [ p ] }$ in Definition 5.3.2 satisfies the following properties:

===== [p167-169] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.4.2 RCR identification criterion (13块) =====
--[p168]-- The worst-case RCR distance from $G$ to any element in D is defined as

--[p169]-- Program (6.20) readily delivers the RCR coprime factors $\{ \hat { N } ( q , \theta ^ { \mathrm { c c } } ) , \hat { D } ( q , \theta ^ { \mathrm { c c } } ) \}$ and the norm-bound on the uncertainty $\| \pmb { \Delta } \| _ { \infty } = d _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( \hat { G } ( q , \theta ^ { \mathrm { c c } } ) )$ , see (6.9), which together form the RCR model set $\mathcal { P } ^ { \mathrm { R C R } }$ via parametrization (6.10).
--[p169]-- The minimization in (6.20) involves the computation of a Chebyshev central model, subject to a stability constraint, which ensures via Lemma 6.3.5 that $\{ \hat { N } , \hat { D } \}$ is coprime, as is required for the dYK parametrization.
--[p168]-- The key point of the transformation in (6.17) is that D is expressed in a coordinate frame that directly relates to the RCR metric d via Lemma 6.3.4, and as such to the performance bound (6.15). Consequently, the optimal nominal model $\hat { G }$ in view of (6.15) is the central model in D, i.e., the model that minimizes the worst-case distance to any element of ${ \mathcal { D } } ,$ as visualized in Figure 6.2(a).

===== [p21-26] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.4.2 Challenges in Identification of Next-generation Systems (39块) =====
--[p23]-- The most common approaches pursue strategies of worst-case optimal design with respect to a model set that includes the true system, e.g., in M˚artensson and Hjalmarsson (2006); Pronzato and Walter (1988); Rojas et al. (2007b). The resulting optimization problem typically involves a dificult to solve infinite-dimensional constraint set (Ben-Tal and Nemirovski, 1998). Typically pursued discretization approaches to obtain a tractable problem, e.g., in Rojas et al.
--[p22]-- From an optimization viewpoint, the presence of element-wise constraints results in a non-convex optimization program, and hence many experiment design approaches that explicitly rely on convex formulations, including the MIMO framework in Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005), do not provide a solution.
At present, no experiment design algorithms are available that can handle element-wise power constraints. This leads to the formulation of the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R1.A.
--[p24]-- In traditional industrial practice, thermo-dynamic systems are often identified using time-domain approaches that typically involve the estimation of parameters in a highly simplified model structure, e.g., a single-parameter firstorder transfer function, from step response measurements.
--[p23]-- Research Challenge R1.C: Robust experiment design for MIMO systems An inherent issue in experiment design for system identification is that the optimal inputs depend on the true but unknown system (Rojas et al., 2011). To resolve this issue, the majority of experiment design approaches, including Bombois et al. (2006); Gevers et al.
--[p21]-- In addition, when dealing with systems with a large number of inputs and outputs, addressing computational eficiency becomes a crucial aspect to obtain a tractable and practically useful approach, which is less the case for SISO systems. This leads to the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R1. Develop an eficient experiment design approach that enables improving model quality for complex systems with many inputs and outputs.
--[p23]-- Common approaches resort to heuristic procedures or design rules, e.g., Schroeder (1970); Van der Ouderaa et al. (1988b). Extension towards complex MIMO systems is complicated due to the heuristic nature of these algorithms. The method in Guillaume et al. (1996) can conceptually handle MIMO systems, but becomes computationally intractable in the presence of many inputs and outputs.
--[p22]-- Multiple aspects related to Research Challenge R1 are investigated in this thesis.   
This leads to the sub-challenges outlined in the following.
Research Challenge R1.A: Input design under element-wise power constraints: Handling system constraints is a necessary aspect in optimal experiment design to achieve bounded inputs and to ensure plant-friendly operation (Rivera et al., 2009). Naturally, OED problems are formulated and solved as constrained optimization problems.
--[p25]-- The main focus has been on normalized coprime factors (Vidyasagar, 1985) due to their relation to robust stability via the ν-gap metric (Vinnicombe, 2001). In Oomen and Bosgra (2012), a specific robust-control-relevant coprime factorization is proposed that enables identifying model sets for high-performance robust control. This factorization is especially beneficial for the considered class of complex systems, as demonstrated in Boeren et al. (2018); Oomen et al. (2013).
--[p21]-- Optimal experiment design (OED) strategies consist of systematic approaches to select the experimental conditions that maximize the accuracy of the resulting model within limited resources. The topic of OED has received significant research interest in the last decades, see e.g., the survey Gevers (2005).
--[p22]-- Develop an eficient optimal experiment design algorithm for the accurate identification of systems with many inputs and outputs subject to element-wise power constraints.
Research Challenge R1.B: Input design under peak-amplitude constraints: For many mechatronic applications, operational limits can be expressed as peak-amplitude constraints on the system input and output signals. Examples are maximum allowable input forces, or stroke limitations in motion platforms.
--[p25]-- The majority of classical system identification approaches, including prediction error identification (Ljung et al., 1987) and FRF identification (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012) are aimed at identifying models that accurately reflect the open-loop system response. However, when the model serves the purpose of control design, the relevant system behavior is the behavior under feedback control, which may impose substantially diferent requirements on the model quality.
--[p25]-- In line with the classical open-loop identification paradigm, identification methods utilized in industrial practice are typically aimed at open-loop model accuracy, and are hence disconnected from their intended control purpose. Such approaches will no longer be suficient to meet the quality standards for the envisioned next-generation systems.
--[p25]-- In addition, these approaches do not yield a model set representation that is compatible with standard robust control synthesis frameworks (Zhou et al., 1996).
To readily obtain model sets for $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ robust control, methods for identification in $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty }$ have been developed in Chen and Gu (2001); Helmicki et al. (1991).
--[p25]-- Research Challenge R3: Identification of model sets for high-performance robust control: Accounting for the end goal of the model in the system identification procedure is crucial to ensure that the model serves its intended purpose (Hjalmarsson, 2005).
--[p24]-- (2014) that consist in the estimation of the system frequency characteristics along with auxiliary variables to explicitly reconstruct the missing parts. The number of auxiliary estimation variables increases with the amount of missing data, and hence the methods become infeasible in the presence of many missing samples, especially for systems with
1
many inputs and outputs.
--[p21]-- For the considered future systems, significant challenges in the identification and the design of experiments arise, which are the result of the complex system properties as outlined in Section 1.3.1. In this section, the research challenges that are related to the contributions in this thesis are outlined.
--[p24]-- A complication that often arises in industrial practice, and especially for slow dynamic systems that require long identification experiments, is that the measurements cannot be collected without interruptions. This leads to missing samples in the data record. The presence of the missing samples cannot be handled by the classical LPM and LRM.
To handle missing data, extensions to the classical LPM have been proposed in Schoukens et al. (2012); Ugryumova et al.
--[p21]-- In particular, the implications of multiple inputs and outputs on the design formulation and the required computational techniques are presently underexposed. Naive extensions of existing techniques developed for SISO systems towards complex multivariable systems potentially lead to overly conservative results. One of the main reasons is that the directionality in MIMO systems (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005) and their associated input signals is not addressed in SISO approaches.
--[p22]-- These limitations must be non-conservatively addressed in experiment design to ensure safe and efective identification experiments.
The presence of peak-amplitude constraints introduces significant complexity in optimal input design procedures. The main reason is that the peak-amplitude of a signal is a highly non-linear, non-convex, and non-smooth function of the spectral magnitudes and the phase (Guillaume et al., 1996).
--[p25]-- At present, the accurate identification of MIMO thermal systems in industrial practice is hampered by the lack of identification approaches that can handle many missing samples. This leads to the formulation of the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R2. Develop an accurate approach for the nonparametric FRF identification approach of systems with predominant transient behavior that can handle many missing samples.
--[p22]-- To achieve a non-conservative experiment design, it is crucial that the constraints, as mathematically formulated in the optimization program, reflect the actual physical limitations of the considered system as good as possible.
For many complex mechatronic systems such as motion stages, the physical limitations are related to specific components, or, elements. An example of such limitation is a bound on the instantaneous power dissipation of a specific actuator.
--[p21]-- In the simplistic, typically ad-hoc, excitation design approaches often pursued in current industrial practice, as discussed in Section 1.4.1, the freedom in the excitation signal design is barely exploited, hence resulting in non-optimal quality of the identified model. Improved experiment design strategies provide a large potential to improve model quality and reduce experimentation time.
--[p23]-- As a result, the majority of input design approaches relying on convex parametrizations of spectral magnitudes (Jansson and Hjalmarsson, 2005) cannot be used.
To mitigate the complexity in solving peak-amplitude-contrained experiment design, various methods focus on solving the related problem of peak-amplitude minimization of signals with a prescribed power spectrum, which is referred to as crest-factor minimization.
--[p21]-- 1.4.2 Challenges in Identification of Next-generation Systems
The achievable performance of next-generation precision systems hinges on the availability of accurate identification approaches that can cope with the system complexities in a time- and cost-efective way. Especially, efective experiment design strategies in combination with eficient identification algorithms are crucial to achieve high model quality within limited experiment time.
--[p26]-- Although the identification of robust-control-relevant coprime factors is a promising step towards achieving the required robust performance for nextgeneration systems, a synergistic framework that exploits the potential of this factorization to connect experiment design, identification, and robust control is presently not available. This leads to the formulation of the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R3.
Develop a coprime-factor based experiment design and identification approach that enables high-performance robust control of complex motion systems.
--[p24]-- Although the identification of mechanical and thermal models is conceptually alike, predominant transient behavior associated to slow thermo-dynamic behavior renders frequency-domain approaches that rely on steady-state data, including the classical empirical transfer function estimate (ETFE) (Ljung et al., 1987), ineficient.
--[p23]-- At present, there are no crest-factor minimization techniques that can eficiently handle the considered classs of complex systems. This leads to the formulation of the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R1.B. Develop an eficient optimal experiment design algorithm for the accurate identification of systems with many inputs and outputs subject to peak-amplitude constraints.
--[p21]-- Modern experiment design typically aims at formulating the excitation design problem as a convex optimization problem, see e.g., Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005), which can readily be solved via convex optimization (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004).
Despite the existence of a large number of theoretical results and experiment design frameworks, the results for the considered class of complex systems are scarce.
--[p23]-- (2003), base their solution on a nominal model obtained from first principles modeling or estimated from a prior identification experiment (Hildebrand and Gevers, 2002). Neglecting uncertainty in the prior model knowledge may lead to violation of the operational limits or unsatisfactory quality of the identified model, especially for the envisioned highly complex systems.
Robust experiment design aims to explicitly address the uncertainties in the prior model.
--[p22]-- For MIMO experiment design problems, dealing with such element-wise constraints is significantly more complicated than for SISO problems, since it requires addressing both the input gains and directions, the latter of which do not exist for SISO systems.
--[p25]-- Especially for the robust inferential control problem in Section 1.3.2, systematic procedures are required that are aimed towards the end goal of robust control performance.
Identification for robust control aims at explicitly identifying models in view of robust control criteria (Oomen and Steinbuch, 2015).
--[p24]-- These techniques include the local polynomial method (LPM) (Pintelon et al., 2010) and the local rational method (LRM) (McKelvey and Gu´erin, 2012), in which smoothness of the local frequency-domain characteristics of the system and transient are exploited to better suppress the efect of transients.
--[p23]-- (2007b), require a large-sized discrete subset, leading to a substantial computational burden (Welsh and Rojas, 2009).

Departing from discretization, in Bombois et al. (2021); M˚artensson and Hjalmarsson (2006) the aim is to replace the infinite-dimensional set constraint by another one that approximates the original constraint. The results are limited to SISO systems and specific design criteria.
--[p25]-- As shown in Vinnicombe (2001, Section 9.5.2), the methods lead to overly large model sets and consequently conservative results.
Motivated by the connections between coprime factor-based uncertainty structures and closed-loop system properties, another line of research has focused on coprime factor identification (Date and Vinnicombe, 2004; Douma and Van den Hof, 2005; Van den Hof et al., 1995).
--[p24]-- At present, experiment design is hampered by a lack of eficient approaches that can robustly handle complex MIMO systems. This leads to the following research challenge.
Research Challenge R1.C. Develop a robust optimal experiment design algorithm for the accurate identification of complex MIMO systems.
--[p24]-- More recently, nonparametric local frequency-domain identification techniques have been developed that can handle transient data, hereby enabling accurate and time-eficient identification of slow thermo-dynamic systems.
--[p25]-- Several results focus on connecting uncertainty regions delivered by classical stochastic identification (Ljung et al., 1987) to robust stability and robust performance metrics, e.g., Bombois et al. (2001); Gevers et al. (2003); Hjalmarsson (2005). These approaches potentially meet with conservative outcomes for complex motion systems due to the use of the ν-gap metric (Vinnicombe, 2001), as shown in Lanzon and Papageorgiou (2009).
--[p24]-- Research Challenge R2: Identification from incomplete data: The increasing importance of active control of thermo-dynamic behavior for the envisioned next-generation systems, as discussed in Section 1.3, results in a growing need for high-quality thermo-dynamic models.
--[p21]-- Research Challenge R1: Experiment design for complex multivariable systems: The accuracy of models obtained from system identification is crucially determined by the quality of the data collected from the identification experiments (Goodwin, 1977). Hence, a good design of the identification experiments is key to obtaining high-quality models. In particular, the excitation signal must be designed such that the generated data carries as much as possible relevant information of the system.

===== [p95-96] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.1 Introduction (12块) =====
--[p95]-- (2021) a separation of graph framework is used to substitute the infinite-dimensional constraint by a diferent one that implies the original constraint. While this enables providing hard robustness guarantees, the substitution comes with conservatism.
Further, the approach is limited to a specific design criterion and single input single output (SISO) systems. In M˚artensson and Hjalmarsson (2006), the infinite-dimensional constraints are preserved by exploiting sum of squares formulations.
--[p95]-- The topic of optimal experiment design has seen much research over the past decades, including Fedorov and Hackl (1997), Goodwin (1977), and more recent surveys Hjalmarsson (2005) and Gevers (2005).
Paradoxically, an inherent issue in experiment design for system identification is that the optimal inputs depend on the true system itself, which is unknown prior to the identification experiment.
--[p95]-- To obtain a tractable approach to solve the robust design problem, several techniques have been developed that involve discretization of the continuous uncertainty set. In Rojas et al. (2007b), the uncertainty set is gridded after which the problem is solved via standard convex optimization. In Welsh and Rojas (2009), a scenario approach is presented wherein the continuous uncertainty set is approximated by a finite number of constraints that are randomly selected.
--[p95]-- Typically, the model set is described as a compact continuous set, by which the design problem becomes a semi-infinite program (SIP), i.e., an optimization problem over a finite number of decision variables but with an infinite-dimensional constraint set. Such problems are generally dificult to solve and potentially NP-hard (Ben-Tal and Nemirovski, 1998). This makes robust experiment design a complicated task in general.
--[p96]-- The aim of this chapter is to present a robust experiment design approach for complex MIMO systems, that does not require the inclusion of a large number of constraints or auxiliary parameters.
The contributions of this chapter are:
1. An iterative robust experiment design algorithm for system identification of multivariable systems, based on the S-lemma.
2. An experimental validation on a multivariable motion system.

--[p95]-- A drawback of these approaches is that the required size of the finite subset is typically large (Welsh and Rojas, 2009), which comes with a substantial computational burden (Hettich and Kortanek, 1993). --[p95]-- 4.1 Introduction
The accuracy of models estimated from system identification depends crucially on the experimental conditions. This applies especially to complex multiple inputs multiple outputs (MIMO) systems such as wafer stages (Dirkx et al., 2020). Optimal experiment design consists in a systematic approach for the design of inputs that maximize model quality within limited experimental resources.
--[p95]-- In the majority of literature, this issue is circumvented by using a (point) estimate of the true system (Barenthin et al., 2008), e.g., in Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005), Bombois et al. (2006). In the presence of uncertainty in the estimated model, such nominal design approaches lack performance guarantees in view of the true system.
The aim in robust experiment design is to explicitly address the uncertainties in the prior model.
--[p95]-- The most widely considered approach is to design inputs that are worst-case optimal with respect to a model set that includes the true system, leading to a min-max design (M˚artensson and Hjalmarsson, 2006; Pronzato and Walter, 1988; Rojas et al., 2007b).

--[p46]-- 2.4.3 Approximate solution to general case: a Sequential Semi-Definite Relaxation (SSDR) algorithm
Besides some special cases, no general methods to exactly solve rank-constrained optimization problems exist (Markovsky, 2008). Therefore, this section presents the SSDR algorithm that aims to solve (RSDP) by approximation. A key property of the SSDR algorithm is that its solution converges to a local optimum of (RSDP).
--[p47]-- However, since the direction matrix W depends directly on the eigenvectors of X, it cannot be computed before X is solved. This dependency is addressed by performing an iterative procedure, wherein the direction matrix ${ \mathcal { W } } ^ { \langle j \rangle }$ at an iteration j is based on the solution $X ^ { \langle j - 1 \rangle }$ obtained in the previous iteration $j - 1$ .
--[p49]-- The proof is presented in Appendix 2.A.4.
2.4.3.3 Discussion: The SSDR algorithm provides a systematic approach to approximately solve the nonlinear and generally hard optimization problem (NLP) using convex optimization tools, e.g., the software package CVX (Grant and Boyd, 2014). Furthermore, convergence to a local optimum of the original problem is guaranteed by Theorem 2.4.7.
--[p46]-- , \lambda _ { 1 } ( X ) \geq . . . \geq \lambda _ { n } ( X )$ then
$$
\sum _ { i = r + 1 } ^ { n } \lambda _ { i } ( X ) = \underset { \mathcal { W } \in \Theta _ { r } } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e ~ } } \langle \mathcal { W } , X \rangle ,\tag{2.17}
$$
where $\Theta _ { r } = \{ \mathcal { W } \in \mathbb { H } _ { + } ^ { n } | \ 0 \preceq \mathcal { W } \preceq I , \ \mathrm { T r } ( \mathcal { W } ) = n - r \}$
Matrix W in (2.17) is referred to as a direction matrix.

--[p49]-- For large-dimensional design problems, however, Algorithm 2.1 may impose a heavy computational burden, as it requires solving a large-dimensional SDP at each iteration. A second drawback of SSDR is that no quantitative performance guarantees can be given a priori, besides the qualitative guarantee of local optimality. In the next section, an alternative method is presented that addresses these two items.

--[p46]-- The key principle behind the approximation of the rank constraint is based on the property that a $n \times n$ matrix of rank r has $n - r$ zero eigenvalues. Therefore, instead of imposing constraints on the rank of a matrix, constraints are imposed onto its $n - r$ eigenvalues. Consider the following lemma.

The algorithm builds upon existing alternating projection based methods in Dattorro (2010); Delgado et al. (2014), and the iterative rank minimization or relaxation methods in Cao et al. (2017); Sun and Dai (2017).

===== [p194-194] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> (3块) =====
--[p194]-- The aim of this chapter is to improve positioning performance at the unmeasured point of interest via a multivariable inferential control design approach. The framework accommodates the use of additional sensors and actuators to mitigate performance limitations that are specific to the non-collocated inferential control problem. In addition, an uncertainty structure for this class of problems is presented that enables achieving high-performance robust control.
--[p194]-- Experimental results from an industrial wafer stage setup confirm that the developed techniques lead to a significant performance improvement at the unmeasured point of interest compared to classical control approaches.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C4 of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. et al. (2023a). Point of interest positioning in high-precision motion systems: a robust inferential control framework. In preparation for journal submission.
--[p194]-- Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: Structural deformations are a key obstruction to achieve the everincreasing performance demands in high-precision positioning systems. This applies especially to systems in which the performance variable, also called the point of interest, is not directly measured and the disturbance inputs are not collocated with the actuated inputs.

===== [p75-76] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.3.1 Gradual smoothing strategy (4块) =====
--[p75]-- with $\bar { z } = [ z _ { 1 } / c _ { 1 } , \ldots , z _ { n _ { y } } / c _ { n _ { z } } ]$ , this problem is formulated as the unconstrained $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ minimization problem over $\Phi$ , i.e.,
$$
\mathrm { m i n i m i z e } \ l _ { \infty } \left( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) \right) .\tag{3.8}
$$
By virtue of (3.1), prior knowledge of $G$ is required to solve (3.8), which is assumed available, e.g., from preliminary identification (Hildebrand and Gevers, 2002).
--[p76]-- Besides obtaining a diferentiable problem, a gradual smoothing strategy reduces the susceptibility to local minima, as exemplified next.
Example 3.3.1. Consider a scalar-valued signal w as in $( 3 . 5 )$ with $N _ { k } = 5 2$ $a _ { 1 k } = \sqrt { 2 / N _ { k } }$ , ∀k and uniformly distributed random phases $\phi _ { 1 k } \in [ 0 , 2 \pi )$ $k =$ $1 , \ldots , N _ { k } - 2$ . The contour maps of $l _ { \infty } ( w )$ and the approximations are shown in Figure 3.2 and Figure 3.3.
The method is formalized in the next section.
--[p75]-- 3.3.1 Gradual smoothing strategy
In this section, the design of the phases $\Phi$ in (3.5) for prescribed spectral magnitudes A is considered, corresponding to subproblem SP2. By using the equivalence
$$
l _ { \infty } ( z _ { p } ) \leq c _ { p } , \forall p \quad \Leftrightarrow \quad l _ { \infty } ( \bar { z } ) \leq 1 ,\tag{3.7}
$$
--[p76]-- The key idea of the presented approach is to solve the non-smooth problem (3.8) via a sequence of approximate smooth problems, wherein the level of smoothness is gradually reduced. A similar strategy is employed in Guillaume et al. (1991), using $l _ { p } { \mathrm { - n o r m } }$ approximations of the $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ to solve (3.8). In this chapter, an exponential smoothing function is employed that achieves accurate approximations.

===== [p96-98] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.2.2 Identification framework (13块) =====
--[p97]-- The covariance error in the estimates $\hat { \theta }$ of any parametric estimator is lower bounded by the Cram´er-Rao lower bound, i.e., cov $\widehat { \theta } \succeq M _ { \theta , o } ^ { - 1 }$ , where $M _ { \theta , o }$ is the Fisher information matrix (Goodwin, 1977). where $\begin{array} { r } { N ( q , \rho ) \ \in \ \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { y } \times n _ { u } } , D ( q , \rho ) \ \in \ \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { u } \times n _ { u } } , N _ { H } ( q , \rho ) \ \in \ \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { y } \times n _ { y } } , D _ { H } ( q , \rho ) \ \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { u } \times n _ { u } } .
--[p98]-- Expression (4.5) shows that the lower bound on the achievable covariance in the parameter estimates is determined by the input spectrum $\Phi _ { u }$ , which motivates the employment of optimal input design strategies.
The key point that is addressed in this chapter is that the bound in (4.5) depends on the true system parameter $\theta _ { o }$ .
--[p97]-- It is assumed that the true system is within the model set, i.e., there exists a parameter $\theta _ { o }$ such that $G ( q , \theta _ { o } ) = G _ { o } ( q )$ and $H ( q , \theta _ { o } ) = H _ { o } ( q )$
The right matrix fraction description (RMFD) parametrization for $G ( q , \rho )$ and $H ( q , \mu )$ is considered,
$$
\begin{array} { l } { { G ( q , \rho ) = N ( q , \rho ) D ^ { - 1 } ( q , \rho ) , } } \\ { { H ( q , \mu ) = N _ { H } ( q , \rho ) D _ { H } ^ { - 1 } ( q , \mu ) .

--[p97]-- Furthermore, $e \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { y } }$ represents measurement noise, characterized as an independent and identically distributed (iid) standard normal random sequence with covariance $\Lambda ,$ and $H _ { o } ( q )$ is a $n _ { y } \times n _ { y }$ stable inversely monic TFM representing the noise dynamics.

--[p98]-- Since this parameter is unknown prior to performing the identification experiments, or in fact may never be known exactly, the optimal input spectrum $\Phi _ { u }$ cannot be computed exactly. Replacing $\theta _ { o }$ with a point estimate, as in done in nominal experiment design, may lead to unsatisfactory results when the input spectrum is applied to the true system.
--[p98]-- In this chapter, worst-case optimal design of the input spectrum $\Phi _ { u }$ is considered that is robust to the uncertainty in the true parameter $\theta _ { o }$

===== [p40-43] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.3 Role of multivariable excitations in OED (9块) =====
