The main point of the comparison above is to show that the optimal excitations have a frequency-wise directionality that depends on the directionality of the systems at hand, which is in sharp contrast to the SIMO and orthogonal excitations. This chapter presents techniques to optimally design such multivariable excitations.
--[p41]-- W ^ { \mathrm { o r t h } } ( k ) = T _ { a } ( k ) T _ { o } ,\tag{2.12}
$$
where $T _ { o }$ is the (orthogonal) DFT matrix composed as
$$
[ T _ { o } ] _ { m , n } = 1 / { \sqrt { n _ { u } } } \mathrm { e } ^ { - j 2 \pi ( m - 1 ) ( n - 1 ) / n _ { u } } ,\tag{2.13}
$$
Basically, the DFT matrix performs a rotation of the excitation directions compared to SIMO excitations. Such rotation is beneficial in the case that power constraints are imposed onto the excitations w.
--[p40]-- 2.3 Role of multivariable excitations in OED
In this section, the role of multivariable excitation design in the identification of MIMO systems is investigated. To this end, the limitations of commonly used traditional excitation techniques are pointed out. These limitations underline the necessity of multivariable approaches.
A traditional approach to performing the $n _ { u }$ identification experiments is to excite a single system input at a time.
--[p41]-- Let $\mathcal { C } _ { Y } = 1$ , by which the cost function in (2.7) simplifies to $\mathcal { I } \left( W \right) = \mathrm { T r } \left( W W ^ { H } \right) ^ { - 1 }$ The best achievable SIMO and orthogonal excitations are visualized in Figure $\it 2 . 4$
Example 2.3.1 shows that orthogonal excitations lead to improved accuracy upon SIMO excitations in the case of excitation power constraints. However, they do not provide a solution for the general case.
--[p40]-- Throughout, this approach will be referred to as the Single Input Multiple Outputs (SIMO) approach. SIMO excitations are of the form,
$$
W ^ { \mathrm { S I M O } } ( k ) = T _ { a } ( k ) ,\tag{2.11}
$$
where $T _ { a } ( k ) \in \mathbb { C } ^ { n _ { u } \times n _ { u } }$ is a diagonal frequency-wise scaling matrix.
--[p41]-- This is illustrated in the following example.
Example 2.3.1 (Limitations of SIMO excitations). Consider the identification problem for a system
$$
G = \left[ \begin{array} { c c } { { 1 } } & { { 0 . 7 } } \\ { { 0 . 8 } } & { { 1 } } \end{array} \right] ,\tag{2.14}
$$
in open-loop, i.e., $S = I$ . Let the constraints be imposed upon the excitations, i.e., $| W _ { i } ^ { [ e ] } | ^ { 2 } \leq 1$ , ∀i, e.
--[p41]-- The above examples illustrate that generally neither of the traditional design techniques lead to optimal performance, because the excitation directions are fixed by design. This limitation is addressed in multivariable excitation design, wherein the structure of $W ( k )$ is unrestricted, enabling optimization of both the excitation magnitudes and directions.
Example 2.3.3 (Multivariable excitations). Consider the same identification problem as in Example 2.3.2.
--[p41]-- The primary drawback of using SIMO excitations is that the MIMO system is treated as a multiple of SIMO systems, and thereby the potential in using the multiplicity of inputs is not exploited.
An alternative traditional approach that does make use of this multiplicity is the so-called orthogonal excitation design approach (Dobrowiecki et al., 2006; Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012, Sec. 2.7.2). Orthogonal excitations are of the form:
$$
--[p41]-- This is illustrated in the following example, wherein output constraints are considered.
Example 2.3.2 (Limitations of orthogonal excitations). The best achievable SIMO and orthogonal excitations are visualized in Figure 2.5. In this case, using orthogonal excitations deteriorates the performance with respect to SIMO excitations.

===== [p98-99] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.2.3 Robust experiment design problem (11块) =====
--[p99]-- Problem (4.9) involves the optimization of the infinite-dimensional decision variable $\Phi _ { u } ( \omega )$ over an infinite-dimensional constraint set $\Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } }$ . In the next section, the decision variable is discretized to obtain a tractable formulation.
--[p98]-- The first measure is the parameter covariance matrix defined as $C _ { \theta } ( \theta _ { o } , \Phi _ { u } ) = M ^ { - 1 } ( \theta _ { o } , \Phi _ { u } )$ , with M given in (4.5). The second measure is the frequency-wise covariance of the identified plant model $C _ { G } ( q , \theta _ { o } , \Phi _ { u } ) = \mathrm { c o v } \left( \mathrm { v e c } ( G ( q , \theta _ { o } ) ) \right)$ , which is estimated using a first-order Taylor approximation as (Jansson and Hjalmarsson, 2005),
--[p99]-- P _ { u } ( \Phi _ { u } ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 \pi } \int _ { - \pi } ^ { \pi } \mathrm { T r } \big ( \Phi _ { u } ( \omega ) \big ) d \omega ,\tag{4.11}
$$
and
$$
P _ { y } ( \theta , \Phi _ { u } ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 \pi } \int _ { - \pi } ^ { \pi } \mathrm { T r } \big ( G ( q , \theta ) \Phi _ { u } ( \omega ) G ^ { H } ( q , \theta ) \big ) d \omega ,
$$
and $w \geq 0$ a user-defined weight.
--[p98]-- 4.2.3 Robust experiment design problem
Optimal experiment design consists in computing an input spectrum $\Phi _ { u }$ that achieves optimal model quality, in some sense, while respecting possible limitations in experimental resources, e.g., bounded inputs or outputs.
In this chapter, two choices of quality measures are considered.
--[p99]-- The goal in this chapter is to design an input spectrum $\Phi _ { u }$ that maximizes the model accuracy in a worst-case sense with respect to the initial uncertainty region (4.8). Specifically, the following robust design problem is considered,
$$
--[p99]-- For the choice $X = G$ , the objective is the minimization of the largest eigenvalue of the worst-case plant frequency-wise covariance, see (4.7). For the choice $X = \theta$ , the objective becomes an E-optimality criterion (Kiefer, 1974).
The constraint (4.10) reflects limited experimental resources, given by the weighted sum of the input power $P _ { u }$ and output power $P _ { y }$ , where
$$
--[p99]-- The key diference between the robust design problem (4.9) and nominal input design is that the constraints in (4.9) are evaluated over the uncertainty set $\Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } }$ that contains the unknown parameter $\theta _ { o } ,$ while in nominal design these are evaluated around a point estimate of $\theta _ { o } .$ The dual ‘least costly’ problem to (4.9) in the E-optimal case (for $X = \theta )$ is considered in Bombois et al. (2021) for a SISO system.
--[p99]-- \begin{array} { r l r l } & { \underset { \Phi _ { u } , \gamma } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e } } \ \gamma } & & { } \\ & { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o } } & & { } \\ & { C _ { X } \left( e ^ { j \omega } , \theta , \Phi _ { u } ( \omega ) \right) \preceq \gamma , \quad \omega \in \Omega , } & & { \theta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } } \\ & { P _ { u } \left( \Phi _ { u } ( \omega ) \right) + w P _ { y } \left( \theta , \Phi _ { u } ( \omega ) \right) \leq 1 , } & & { \theta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } , } \end{array}\tag{4.9}
$$
(4.10)
where $X \in \{ \theta , G \}$ and $\Omega = ( - \pi , \pi ]$ and w is a user-selected weight.
--[p98]-- In this chapter, it is assumed that the true parameter $\theta _ { o }$ is unknown, but is known to lie with α-probability within the ellipsoidal region
$$
\Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } = \big \{ \theta : \varepsilon ^ { T } M _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } \ : \varepsilon \leq \chi _ { \alpha } ^ { 2 } ( n _ { \theta } ) \big \} ,\tag{4.8}
$$
--[p99]-- with $\varepsilon : = \theta - \theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } }$ , and $\chi _ { \alpha } ^ { 2 } ( n _ { \theta } )$ denoting the α-probability level for a $\chi ^ { 2 }$ distribution with $n _ { \theta }$ degrees of freedom. Uncertainty regions described by (4.8) are delivered by classical stochastic identification approaches, e.g., the prediction error method (Ljung et al., 1987).
--[p98]-- $$
C _ { G } ( \ v q , \ u _ { \theta _ { o } } , \Phi _ { u } ) = \Lambda _ { G } ( \ v q , \ u _ { \theta _ { o } } ) C _ { \theta } ( \ v \theta _ { o } , \Phi _ { u } ) \Lambda _ { G } ^ { H } ( \ v q , \ u _ { \theta _ { o } } ) .\tag{4.7}
$$
An inherent property of input spectrum design problems in general is that the optimal spectrum depends on the true but unknown system parameter $\theta _ { o }$ , as reflected by (4.7).

--[p79]-- 3.3.4 A descent algorithm
This section presents Algorithm 3.3, that enables accurately solving (3.8) by a joint minimization of the smoothing function $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ and a gradual decrease of the smoothing parameter. The algorithm bears resemblance to that in Xu (2001) for solving minmax problems, but difers in the selection of the search direction and the control of the smoothing parameter.
--[p80]-- Properties ii) and iii) in Lemma 3.3.2 enable the following result.
Theorem 3.3.4. Let $\{ L ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } ) \}$ be the sequence generated by Algorithm 3.3 for $i \to \infty$ . Then,
(i) The sequence $\{ L ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } ) \}$ is monotonically non-increasing.
(ii) The point $\Phi ^ { \infty }$ is a stationary point of $l _ { \infty } ( \bar { z } ( A , \Phi ) )$
Proof.
--[p80]-- Property ii) in Lemma 3.3.2 implies that a stationary point of ${ \cal L } ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } )$ is a stationary point $l _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } { \left( \bar { z } ( \Phi ^ { i } ) , \sigma ^ { i } \right) }$ for $\sigma ^ { i }  0 ,$ ， which is formalized in (Xu, 2001, Theorem 3.1). □
The convergence properties in Theorem 3.3.4 are preserved for any other parameter update in step 5 that reduces $L$ in each iteration.
--[p80]-- Thus, by property iii) in Lemma 3.3.2, $L ( \Phi ^ { i + 1 } , \sigma ^ { i + 1 } ) \leq L ( \Phi ^ { i + 1 } , \sigma ^ { i } ) \leq L ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } )$ . (ii) For fixed $\sigma ^ { i } > 0$ , steps 2-5 guarantee that ${ \cal L } ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } )$ is reduced towards a stationary point for $i \to \infty$ . Step 6 guarantees reduction of $\sigma$ by $q \gets 1$ when a stationary point is suficiently approached. Hence, $\sigma ^ { i }  0$ for $i  \infty$ .
--[p80]-- (i) For fixed $\sigma ^ { i } > 0 , - d ^ { i }$ in step 2 is a descent direction of $\tilde { L }$ and $L ,$ where the latter follows from (3.16). Step 3 guarantees that $L ( \Phi ^ { i + 1 } , \sigma ^ { i } ) \ \leq$ ${ \cal L } ( \Phi ^ { i } , \sigma ^ { i } )$ . Step 6 guarantees that $\sigma ^ { i + 1 } \leq \sigma ^ { i }$ .

===== [p49-53] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.4.4 Approximate solution to general case: a Relaxation and Randomization (RR) approach (27块) =====
--[p50]-- T = \left[ \begin{array} { l l l } { \frac { 1 } { \underset { i } { \operatorname* { m a x } } \mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ 1 ] } } } & & \\ & { \ddots } & \\ & & { \ddots } & \\ & & { \frac { 1 } { \underset { i } { \operatorname* { m a x } } \mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ n _ { u } ] } } } \end{array} \right] .\tag{2.23}
$$
Based on this principle, an eficient algorithm for approximately solving (NLP) is presented in Algorithm 2.2.
--[p50]-- 2.4.4.2 Performance analysis: In this section, the performance of the RR algorithm is quantified by means of a statistics-based performance analysis. By constructing $R ( k )$ in (2.22) according to Procedure 2.4.8, the element-wise powers $\mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] }$ in (2.21) become random variables with specific stochastic properties that are determined by the solution of (SDR ). This is shown in the following lemma.
Lemma 2.4.9.
--[p51]-- Theorem 2.4.10 shows that the achievable performance depends on the problem dimension via parameters $n _ { u } , n _ { \xi }$ , and N, where the latter enters (2.26) through the variance $\sigma ^ { 2 } \{ \mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] } \}$ , see Lemma 2.4.9. This dependency is visualized by means of a numerical example. The global trends are, however, representative for many (real) systems in general.
Example 2.4.11 (Worst-case approximation bound).
--[p52]-- This trend is explained by the ratio between the number of constraints and the number of decision variables: for increasing N, the number of decision variables increases while the number of constraints remains equal, hence the solution space increases. In contrast, for an increasing input dimension, both the number of decision variables and the number of constraints increase.
In many practical identification problems, the number of excitation frequencies N is large.
For large N, a substantially tighter bound than (2.26) can be formulated, under mild conditions. Consider first the following lemma:
Lemma 2.4.12.
--[p50]-- A feasible set of excitations $W .$ , i.e., one that satisfies $\mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] } \le 1 , \forall i , e .$ is generated by choosing $W ( k )$ as
$$
W ( k ) = \Delta ^ { \dagger } ( k ) R ( k ) \sqrt { T } ,\tag{2.22}
$$
with
$$
--[p50]-- Let $R ( k ) , \forall k$ be chosen as a random Haar unitary. Then, the element-wise powers $\mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] }$ in (2.21) are random variables with expected value
$$

--[p49]-- 2.4.4 Approximate solution to general case: a Relaxation and Randomization (RR) approach

--[p49]-- 2.4.4.1 RR algorithm: The key idea behind the RR method is to extract an approximate solution to (NLP) from the solution of the convex relaxation (SDR) in a randomized fashion. Even though the idea of a randomizationbased approach is simple, many theoretically worst-case approximation bounds for semi-definite relaxation techniques are proven through randomization.
--[p51]-- Likewise, by choosing the excitations W according to (2.22), the achieved cost $f _ { j }$ of Algorithm 2.2 becomes a random variable with stochastic properties determined by those of $\mathcal { P } _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { [ e ] }$ . This gives rise to the formulation of a theoretically worst-case approximation bound of (SDR), which is a measure of the achievable performance of Algorithm 2.2:
Theorem 2.4.10.
--[p53]-- Finally, the achieved performance using RR typically improves for identification problems with a larger number of excitation frequencies, which is particularly beneficial for many real-life identification problems.
--[p53]-- This indicates the practical value of Theorem 2.4.13.
2.4.4.3 Discussion: The RR algorithm has several advantages over SSDR. First, it requires solving a SDP only once instead of at each iteration, by which the computational complexity is reduced substantially compared to SSDR. Furthermore, the RR approach provides a priori performance indicators, which is not the case for the SSDR algorithm, besides the local optimality guarantees formulated in Theorem 2.4.7.

===== [p226-226] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.8 Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p226]-- 7.8 Conclusions
The methods presented in this chapter enable improving the performance of highprecision motion systems beyond classical control approaches. This is achieved by a multivariable inferential control approach that explicitly incorporates the dynamics between the performance variables and the measured variables, and between the actuated inputs and the disturbances in the controller design, to improve the positioning at the unmeasured point of interest.
--[p226]-- Performance limitations in the non-collocated inferential control problem are characterized and addressed in the control design via a multivariable weighting filter design approach. To achieve robustness with respect to model uncertainty, a coprime factorization-based approach for the construction of robust-inferential-controlrelevant model sets is developed that enables high-performance robust control.
--[p226]-- The presented techniques are verified both in simulation and in an experimental validation on a wafer stage setup. The results show a significant performance enhancement using the presented techniques compared to classical approaches.

===== [p234-236] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 8.1 Conclusions (15块) =====
--[p234]-- 8.1 Conclusions
The advanced identification and control methods developed in this thesis enable reaching new levels of precision in semiconductor manufacturing equipment. The developed techniques aim at enhancing positioning performance by improving the whole chain of control systems design, from the design of experiments to the acquisition of data, from data to the estimation of models, and from models to robust controllers design.
--[p235]-- The approach does not involve the explicit estimation or reconstruction of the missing data parts and hence does not require the estimation of auxiliary variables, in contrast to most existing algorithms for dealing with missing samples. Experimental validation on a thermo-dynamical experimental setup with predominant transient behavior confirms that the method achieves accurate FRF identification.
--[p235]-- Experimental confrontation with a MIMO flexible beam setup shows that the method significantly improves robustness compared to nominal design approaches.
The second main contribution of this thesis, in Chapter 5, is the development of an approach for the non-parametric FRF identification in the presence of missing samples.
--[p236]-- A $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ optimal controller design approach is pursued that incorporates the dynamics between the performance variables and the measured variables, and between the actuated inputs and the disturbances. Multivariable aspects are explicitly addressed in a directional weighting filter approach that accomodates the use of over-sensing and over-actuation strategies to mitigate sensor- and actuator-related performance limitations.
--[p235]-- Experimental results from a $7 \times 8$ wafer stage show a significant improvement of FRF quality compared to traditional approaches, and confirm that the developed computational techniques are suitable for application to large-dimensional industrial equipment.
In Chapter 3, an eficient algorithm is developed that enables generation of peakamplitude constrained excitation signals with a prescribed power spectrum for systems with many inputs and outputs.
--[p235]-- In Chapter 4, an experiment design algorithm is developed that is robust to initial model parameter uncertainty described by ellipsoidal regions. The method exploits the S-lemma to avoid discrete approximations of the continuous uncertainty set, and to avoid the need for including a large number of scenarios in the optimization program, both of which are typical in the majority of existing approaches.
--[p234]-- By efectively dealing with these complexities, the presented methods provide an important contribution towards creating the next generation of semiconductor manufacturing systems that will power our increasingly digital world.
original problem to eficiently obtain an approximately optimal solution.
--[p235]-- It is shown that combining the multivariable peak minimization algorithm with the multivariable input spectrum design methods developed in Chapter 2 gives rise to a highly eficient and powerful two-step optimal experiment design framework for FRF identification. This is confirmed by experimental results from an $1 8 ~ \times$ 6 active vibration isolation system.
--[p235]-- The method consists in a two-stage approach to approximate the Chebyshev central model within a robust-control-relevant-uncertainty region. In addition, a coprime-factor-based experiment design algorithm is developed that is aimed explicitly at minimizing this region, thereby tightly connecting to the presented identification algorithm.
--[p235]-- The method exploits the wavelet-convolution to extend the existing local polynomial method to a wavelet-based method that enables isolating the efect of the missing samples in the time-frequency plane. It is shown that the wavelets can be employed to obtain a time-invariant estimator or a time-varying estimator, which provide diferent mechanisms to address the missing samples, and lead to diferent performance in terms of bias and variance errors.
--[p235]-- The optimization-based algorithm uses a sequence of smooth approximations of the non-smooth objective function to achieve a high level of robustness against local minima. By exploiting the specific structural properties in the gradient and Hessian matrix, the method significantly reduces the computation load compared to existing techniques.
--[p234]-- Herein, the complex interrelations between the diferent design steps are addressed explicitly to establish a synergistic framework for experiment design, system identification, and robust control.
Envisaging the increasing complexity of future precision manufacturing equipment, the tools developed in this thesis are especially geared towards complex mechatronic systems with pronounced flexible and transient dynamics, and a large number of sensors and actuators.
--[p235]-- The third main contribution of this thesis is the development of an identification and experiment design framework for the identification of model sets for highperformance robust control, in Chapter 6. The identification problem is aimed at optimally identifying specific non-normalized coprime factors that transparently connect to non-conservative measures of robust stability and performance.
--[p235]-- Experimental results from a prototype wafer stage demonstrate that the presented techniques lead to small uncertainty sets for robust control, thereby enabling improved robust control performance.
In Chapter 7, a framework for robust inferential control of systems with unmeasured performance variables and non-collocated inputs is presented, which constitutes the fourth main contribution of this thesis.
--[p236]-- In addition, a method for the construction of robust-inferentialcontrol-relevant model sets is developed that enable high-performance robust control for systems with non-collocated inputs and outputs. Good performance is verified on a prototype wafer stage setup, where the presented techniques lead to significantly improved positioning at the unmeasured point of interest compared to classical control approaches.

--[p109]-- Overall, this case study demonstrates that Algorithm 4.4 provides a viable approach to robustify input spectrum design. ===== [p94-94] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> (3块) =====
--[p94]-- In the presented approach, the robust design problem is cast as an infinite-dimensional min-max optimization problem, and tackled via the S-lemma in an iterative approximation scheme. Experimental results from a multivariable motion system show that the algorithm enables significant robustness improvements.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C1.C of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. et al. (2023d).
--[p94]-- Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: Optimal input design plays an important role in system identification for complex and multivariable systems. A known paradox in input design is that the optimal inputs depend on the true but unknown system. The aim of this chapter is to design inputs for multivariable systems that are robust to all system variations within a given continuous uncertainty set.
--[p94]-- Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO system identification via the S-lemma. In In 2023 7th IEEE Conference on Control Technology and Applications (CCTA), Bridgetown, Barbados.

===== [p73-74] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.2.1 Experiment design for system identification (7块) =====
--[p74]-- The experiment design problem considered in this chapter consists in the computation of optimal excitation signals w under $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constraints:
$$
\begin{array} { c } { w ^ { \mathrm { o p t } } = \displaystyle \operatorname* { m i n i m i z e } \mathcal { I } ( P , w ) } \\ { w } \\ { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o ~ } l _ { \infty } ( z _ { p } ) \leq c _ { p } , \quad p = 1 , \dots , n _ { z } } \end{array} ,\tag{3.3}

Definition 3.2.1. The $l _ { \infty }$ -norm of a scalar-valued signal $x ( n )$ is defined as its absolute peak value in the interval $[ 0 , N - 1 ]$ ,

$$

The result of this selection is twofold. First, the regressors $\bar { K } ^ { [ e ] }$ in (5.24) become zero at the even lines $[ 0 , 2 \gamma P , 4 \gamma P , \ldots , ( N - \gamma ) P ]$ . Hence, $Z _ { \Delta }$ in Theorem 5.4.2 can attain non-zero values at these lines only.
--[p128]-- 5.4.3 Time-invariant wavelet-based estimator (TIE)
The main idea of the time-invariant wavelet-based estimator (TIE) is to identify the FRF in the time-frequency plane from the data associated to set $\mathcal { D } ^ { [ e , p ] }$ in (5.11). In this section, the method is formalized, and its properties in view of bias and variance errors are analyzed. This section constitutes contributions 1a) and 2 of this chapter.
--[p132]-- Consider the scenario of Example 5.3.6, for wavelets $\psi _ { \tilde { q } } ^ { [ p ] }$ for $p = 0 , 1 , 2$ . To visualize the achieved approximate diagonality of the matrix $\bar { B }$ in (5.38), its cross-diagonal entries are depicted in Figure 5.8. For increasing values of $p _ { z }$ , the cross-diagonal entries of B<sup>¯</sup> reduce significantly.
--[p131]-- The second inequality in (5.35) indicates that using wavelets with good frequency-domain localization properties (large $p )$ leads to an increase in variance errors. This is due to property c) in Lemma 5.3.3, which states that the support of the wavelet function becomes larger for increasing $p ,$ which in turn reduces the cardinality of the set $\mathcal { D } ^ { [ p ] }$ , leading to a smaller $P _ { \mathrm { T I E } } ^ { [ p ] }$ in (5.36).
--[p129 [表]]-- The second subproblem is aimed at estimating $G _ { z }$ at the even EF lines $\bar { k } _ { \mathrm { e v e n } } =$ $\gamma k _ { \mathrm { e v e n } } P$ with $k _ { \mathrm { e v e n } } = [ 2 , 4 , \dots , \frac { N } { 2 \gamma } - 2 ]$ . The selection is analogous to the first subproblem.

Theorem 5.4.3 and Corollary 5.4.4 show that frequency-domain localizing wavelets negatively afect variance errors. However, frequency-domain localization provides an important instrument to attenuate bias errors, as is shown next.
--[p132]-- Example 5.4.5 and (5.38) indicate that achieving approximate diagonality of $\bar { B }$ requires wavelets with good frequency-domain localization properties and thus large values of $p .$ .
--[p132]-- This observation, together with Corollary 5.4.4, shows that the minimization of variance on the one hand, and of bias on the other hand require the opposite wavelets in terms of parameter $p .$ This once more illustrates the intrinsic trade-of between time- and frequency-domain accuracy in wavelet selection, as discussed in Section 5.3.2.
--[p134]-- The dominant computation in the missing data approach (Ugryumova et al., 2014) is in the QR-factorization of a matrix which size depends linearly on $n _ { m }$ , resulting in a cubic complexity in terms of $n _ { m }$ . In the concatenated data approach (Schoukens et al., 2012), a number of N<sup>˜</sup> systems of equations with dimensionality $n _ { g } n _ { \Theta }$ is solved, resulting in a cubic complexity in terms of $n _ { g }$ .

--[p134 [表]]-- For the wavelet-based LPM approach, the pattern of the missing data appears in the form of the weighting matrix W in (5.38), by which the computational complexity is independent of the number of missing samples. The wavelet-based LPM requires the computation of the DFT of $\ddot { N }$ wavelet functions $\psi _ { j }$ . This computation is performed once and is done eficiently using the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT), resulting in a complexity of $\tilde { N } \bar { N }$ log(N<sup>¯</sup> ).

===== [p14-16] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.2 Next-generation Mechatronics in Semiconductor Manufacturing (10块) =====
--[p15]-- Pursuing such integral control strategy enables leveraging existing designs to increased performance levels, and allows relaxing the demands for new mechanical system design. As such, the mechatronic system design approach may give rise to significantly diferent system architectural choices.
An example of a next-generation mechatronic system design is shown in Figure 1.3(b) (Evers, 2021).
--[p14]-- The mechatronics design approach is crucial for keeping up with the extreme performance demands in the design of high-precision equipment.
Performance requirements for precision manufacturing systems are typically expressed in terms of productivity and accuracy. The capability of these systems to meet these requirements is determined by their ability to, as precise as possible, manipulate or measure a product or substrate, in the presence of environmental disturbances.
--[p15]-- Assuming rigid-body behavior allows standard control techniques to be applied, which consist in system decoupling using static geometric relations, followed by single input single output (SISO) controller design for each of the motion degrees-of-freedom (DOF) (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005). In addition, passive cooling approaches are applied to mitigate temperature fluctuations (Evers, 2021).
--[p14]-- This requires precision systems to behave in a highly deterministic fashion, which in turn demands a high level of insensitivity to non-deterministic disturbances and perturbations (Schmidt et al., 2020).
To achieve precise and deterministic behavior, traditional system design approaches have been focused on passive solutions in combination with excellent mechanical design.
--[p16]-- The performance of future precision systems as exemplified in Figure 1.3(b) hinges on the availability of approaches and algorithms to model, predict, and control the complex system behavior. To sustain the quest for increased precision, there are several key challenges that must be addressed. These are discussed in the next section.
--[p16]-- The system comprises of several mechatronic subsystems, including positioning stages and opto-mechatronic devices. Exploiting a large number of sensors and actuators, the components are integrally positionand temperature-controlled by a centralized multiple inputs multiple outputs (MIMO) controller. By employing advanced control that can cope with the complex system behavior, the demand for mechanical stifness has been relaxed, allowing for lightweight and flexible mechanical stage design.
--[p14]-- 1.2 Next-generation Mechatronics in Semiconductor Manufacturing
The concept of mechatronics has become ubiquitous in the precision manufacturing industry over the last decades. Mechatronics system design is described as “the philosophy in engineering technology in which there is a co-ordinated, and concurrently developed, integration of mechanical engineering with electronics and intelligent computer control in the design and manufacture of products and processes” (Bolton, 2008).
--[p14]-- Such approaches include stif design of mechanical structures to reduce deformations due to external disturbances, passive vibration isolation of performance-critical components, and passive cooling to mitigate temperature fluctuations (Schmidt et al., 2020).
This traditional design approach is illustrated for a wafer stage positioning system in Figure 1.3(a), which reflects the early lithography tool in Figure 1.2(a).
--[p15]-- The lithography goal is to expose the photoresistive layer on the wafer to a miniaturized image of the pattern that is encoded in the reticle. This leads to an imprint of the pattern into the wafer. For a succesful exposure, the wafer stage must position the wafer as precise as possible with respect to the laser beam. To achieve high precision, the stage is traditionally designed as a highly stif structure such that its dynamics resemble that of a rigid body (Van de Wal et al., 2002).
--[p15]-- Pursuing traditional design strategies, the achieved performance relies heavily on mechanical properties and passive suppression of mechanical and thermal disturbances.
For future precision equipment, it is envisaged that passive solutions are no longer suficient to meet the performance requirements. Hence, these passive solutions will be extended towards active mechatronic solutions that synergistically combine sensors, actuators, and software towards an integral control approach.

===== [p59-59] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.6 Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p59]-- This improvement is supported by experimental results from a $7 \times 8$ wafer stage, which show a significantly enhanced FRF quality compared to traditional approaches. Furthermore, the results demonstrate that the developed computational techniques are fast and are suitable for application to large-dimensional realistic systems.
--[p59]-- 2.6 Conclusions
The identification of FRFs of mechatronic systems can be made more accurate and/or faster through the multivariable experiment design framework presented in this chapter. The developed methods improve upon traditional excitation design techniques by exploiting the design freedom in the multivariable excitation signals, while eficiently dealing with the element-wise constraints of the system.

===== [p17-19] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.3.2 Control Challenges for Future Precision Equipment (16块) =====
--[p17]-- 1.3.2 Control Challenges for Future Precision Equipment
The increasing complexity and performance requirements for next-generation precision systems impose significant challenges for control. A recent overview of challenges and developments for control of motion systems is given in Oomen (2018). In this section, the challenges within the scope of this thesis are outlined, where the main focus is on control of flexible motion systems.
--[p19]-- Summary: Control challenges for future precision systems: Summarizing the above discussions, the challenges associated to the considered class of complex systems are due to:
• A large number of inputs and outputs.
• Non-collocation of performance variables with measured variables, and non-collocation of disturbance inputs with actuated inputs.
• Complex dynamic behavior, e.g., due to many lightly damped mechanical resonance phenomena.
--[p18]-- Thermo-mechanical control: Thermal aspects play an increasingly important role for achieving the required performance in next-generation precision systems. On the one hand, this is due to the envisioned increase in productivity, which leads to more dissipation of power, e.g., due to increasing actuator power consumption to perform aggressive motion tasks (Van Es et al., 2018), or due to increasing laser power for improved optical imaging (Van Schoot et al., 2017).
--[p19]-- Indeed, thermomechanical control problems can typically be described by non-collocated inferential control problems, whereas the presence of thermal actuators and sensors in a motion control system can be interpreted as an over-sensing and over-actuation configuration. Distinct challenges in thermo-mechanical control include dealing with multi-physical dynamical properties and diferent time-scales.
--[p17]-- To enable high performance at the point of interest, a model of the flexible dynamics between the point of interest and the non-collocated sensors must be explicitly included in the control design (Oomen et al., 2014). Similarly, a model of the flexible dynamics between the actuators and the non-collocated disturbance inputs must be incorporated.
--[p17]-- Due to the explicit dependence on a model, a crucial aspect herein is the development of robust control techniques that can non-conservatively deal with the non-collocated inferential configuration.
Over-actuation and over-sensing: The presence of spatially distributed disturbances often leads to complex deformations in flexible mechanical structures, which complicates achieving high-performance control.
--[p17]-- Under such rigid-body assumption, the performance variables can be estimated from the measurements using a static transformation from the sensors to the point of interest. For the envisioned future motion systems, such approach is no longer suficient since the rigid-body reasoning becomes invalid for lightweight mechanical structures with predominant flexible behavior.
--[p18]-- While these existing approaches enable high-bandwidth rigid-body control, they do not neccesarily lead to improved performance in the non-collococated inferential control setting, since the spatio-temporal behavior of the point of interest is not explicitly addressed in the control approach. A key challenge involves the development of suitable over-sensing and over-actuation strategies for the non-collocated inferential control configuration to improve the performance at the point of interest.
--[p18]-- On the other hand, the future lightweight structures are potentially more subsceptible to thermal influences than current traditional designs. Resulting temperature gradients may give rise to complexly shaped deformations which can significantly impact the machine performance (Heck et al., 2020).
Departing from the traditional reliance on passive solutions to deal with thermal efects, future systems will exploit active methods to estimate, predict, and
1
--[p18]-- When additional sensors and/or actuators are available, diferent strategies can be pursued towards an improved performance. In Silvas et al. (2011), the sensors and actuators are used to achieve unobservability and/or uncontrollability of a selection of resonant modes, which leads to an improved rigid-body approximation. In Van Herpen et al. (2014), the rigid-body behavior is enhanced by using the additional sensors and actuators to efectively stifen and dampen the mechanical structure.
--[p19]-- • Complex dynamic behavior, e.g., due to significant transient behavior related to thermo-dynamics.
• Multi-physical behavior, e.g., in thermo-mechanical or opto-mechanical systems.
• High performance requirements, expressed in terms of accuracy, productivity, and cost-efectiveness.
The availability of high-quality dynamic models that accurately describe these complex systems is indispensable for precision control.
Challenges in the identification of such models are discussed in the next section.
--[p17]-- Non-collocation of the inputs and the outputs has significant impact on the structure and the design of the control system (Hong and Bernstein, 1998), and as such the traditional motion control design rules can no longer be used. A key challenge lies in the development of a model-based design framework for high-performance inferential controllers.
--[p17]-- Examples are shown in Figure 1.3, where the performance variable is the displacement of the point of exposure on top of the wafer, whereas the stage position is measured at the sides of the stage.
In traditional motion control, the presence of flexible dynamics between the point of interest and the sensors is neglected (Van de Wal et al., 2002).
--[p18]-- This applies particularly to inferential control problems, where there is a discrepancy between the point of interest and the point of measurement. The use of additional sensors, referred to as over-sensing, can be exploited to obtain an improved estimate of the system’s spatio-temporal deformation behavior. Similarly, additional actuators can be used, referred to over-actuation, for improved control of the deformations.
--[p19]-- A key control challenge is the development of advanced methods that combine thermal and mechanical actuators and sensors with smart control algorithms. Partly, these developments may benefit from the aforementioned concepts of inferential control and over-actuation and over-sensing.
--[p17]-- Inferential control: In many motion systems, the point of interest, i.e., the point where performance is required, does not coincide with the location of the sensors. This leads to a situation in which the performance variables cannot be measured directly, but must be inferred from the sensory information.

As a result of fixing these variables, the function $\tilde { f } ^ { [ x ] }$ in (4.30) becomes convex in $\tilde { \Psi } .$ .
--[p104]-- However, the complexity that arises in view of input design (4.15) is that these functions are non-linear and non-convex functions of the inputs $\tilde { \Psi }$ .
--[p104]-- 4.3.4 Robust experiment design algorithm
In this section, the robust experiment design algorithm is presented to approximately solve (4.20).
The approximations $\tilde { f } , \tilde { g }$ in (4.26), (4.28) are quadratic in $\varepsilon ,$ which enables evaluation of the conditions $\tilde { f } \ge 0 , \tilde { g } \ge 0$ over the ellipsoidal set $\Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } }$ , by virtue of Lemma 4.3.2.

--[p104]-- The reasons are twofold: 1) the worst-case parameters $\theta _ { f _ { \mathrm { W C } } }$ and $\theta _ { g _ { \mathrm { W C } } }$ are non-convex functions of $\tilde { \Psi }$ , and 2) the eigenvectors ${ \mathcal { U } } ( q , \theta _ { f _ { \mathrm { W C } } } , { \tilde { \Psi } } )$ in (4.25) are non-convex in $\theta _ { f _ { \mathrm { W C } } }$ and $\tilde { \Psi }$ .

===== [p203-204] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.3.1 Implications of non-collocation on controller structure and dynamics (11块) =====
--[p204]-- A second important implication of noncollocation is that perfect setpoint tracking is not achieved by the controller $C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , d }$ . Instead, achieving $r \mapsto e _ { z } = 0$ in (7.11) requires the controller
$$
C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , r } = ( P _ { z u } - P _ { y u } ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{7.13}
$$
--[p204]-- The above discussion illustrates that the classical single-DOF controller structure is inadequate for the inferential control problem with non-collocated inputs and outputs. This deficiency is resolved by employing a two-DOF controller structure (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005)
$$
u _ { p } = \underbrace { \left[ C _ { r } \phantom { e } C _ { y } \right] } _ { = C } \left[ - y _ { p } \right] ,\tag{7.14}
$$
--[p204]-- In the special cases wherein the disturbance inputs $d _ { p }$ coincide with the control inputs $u _ { p }$ , and/or the performance variables $z _ { p }$ coincide with the measured outputs $y _ { p }$ , the zero-spillover controllers render $C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , d } = \infty , \ C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , r } = \infty$ and hence recover high-gain controllers.
--[p204]-- where $S _ { i } = ( I + C _ { y } P _ { y u } ) ^ { - 1 }$ . Assuming square and invertible entries $P _ { * \circ }$ , it can be shown that perfect disturbance rejection and reference tracking is jointly achieved by a zero-spillover controller with two-DOF structure (7.14). In the next section, the concepts of zero-spillover control are generalized towards minimal-spillover control for systems with non-square entries $P _ { * \mathrm { { o } } }$ .
This gives rise to an analysis framework for fundamental performance limitations.
7
--[p203]-- A further crucial observation is that this is achieved by a high-gain controller C, regardless of the specific system P.
This is in sharp contrast to the inferential control problem that aims at minimization of $e _ { z }$ . The reasons are twofold. First, pursuing a high-gain control strategy does not lead to a minimization of the error $e _ { z }$ . Instead, supposing that all entries $P _ { * \circ }$ are square and invertible, perfect disturbance suppression, $\mathrm { i . e .
--[p204]-- leading to the interconnection in 7.4(b), with closed-loop TFM $\begin{array} { r l } { T _ { e _ { z } } } & { { } = } \end{array}$ $[ T _ { e _ { z } r } \ T _ { e _ { z } d _ { p } } ]$
$$
\left[ T _ { e _ { z } r } T _ { e _ { z } d } \right] : \left[ { r _ { z } } \right] \mapsto e _ { z } = - P _ { z u } S _ { i } \left[ C _ { r } - C _ { y } P _ { y d } \right] + \left[ \begin{array} { l l } { I } & { - P _ { z d } } \end{array} \right] ,\tag{7.15}
$$
--[p203]-- 7.3.1 Implications of non-collocation on controller structure and dynamics
The presence of non-collocated inputs in the inferential control problem imposes diferent requirements on the controller structure and its dynamics than the classical control problem. Consider the feedback interconnection with the classical controller structure in Figure 7.4(a), with transfer function matrix (TFM)
$$
--[p203]-- T : \ \left[ { \bf \Phi } _ { d _ { p } } ^ { r } \right] \mapsto \left[ { \bf \Phi } _ { e _ { y } } ^ { e _ { z } } \right] = \left[ \begin{array} { c } { { - P _ { z u } C \ } } \\ { { I \ } } \end{array} \right] S _ { o } [ \ I \ - P _ { y d } ] + \left[ \begin{array} { c c } { { I \ } } & { { - P _ { z d } } } \\ { { 0 \ } } & { { 0 \ } } \end{array} \right] ,\tag{7.11}
$$
where $\boldsymbol { S _ { o } } = ( \boldsymbol { I } + P _ { y u } \boldsymbol { C } ) ^ { - 1 }$ .
--[p203]-- , }$ $d _ { p } \mapsto e _ { z } = 0$ in (7.11), is achieved by the zero-spillover controller $C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , d }$ (Hong
and Bernstein, 1998),
$$
C ^ { \mathrm { z s } , d } = ( P _ { y d } P _ { z d } ^ { - 1 } P _ { z u } - P _ { y u } ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{7.12}
$$
Evidently, disturbance rejection in the inferential control problem requires a controller with system-specific dynamics that depend on all four entries $P _ { * \circ } ,$ rather than a high-gain controller.
--[p204]-- Since this controller is clearly diferent from (7.12), this implies that joint disturbance rejection and reference tracking can generally not be achieved by the classical controller structure in the presence of non-collocated inputs and outputs.
Remark 7.3.1. The zero-spillover controllers (7.12) and (7.13) are often nonproper and non-stabilizing. Therefore, these serve the purpose of facilitating design and analysis rather than actual implementation.
Remark 7.3.2.
--[p203]-- In classical servo control, the goal is to minimize the error $e _ { y } = r - y _ { p }$ . This control problem consists of a disturbance suppression and a reference tracking problem, which entails the joint minimization of the transfer functions $d _ { p } \mapsto e _ { y }$ and $r \mapsto e _ { y }$ , respectively. From (7.11), it is immediate that minimizing $S _ { o }$ also minimizes $e _ { y }$ .

--[p208]-- 7.4.1 Inferential control goal
The design of $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ optimal controllers requires the selection of the generalized inputs and outputs such that the control criterion $\mathcal { I }$ in $( 7 . 4 )$ reflects the control goal. --[p209]-- In the next section, a method for the design of multivariable weighting filters W and V is presented that reflects desired performance requirements and addresses the performance limitations in multivariable inferential control discussed in Section 7.3.
--[p208]-- The presented selection is shown in Figure 7.7 and is motivated as follows: the inputs $r _ { z }$ and $d _ { p }$ are the inputs that must be tracked or suppressed, respectively. The input $d _ { y }$ is included to enable high-frequency controller roll-of properties, and $d _ { u }$ is included to bound the input sensitivity function $S _ { i } , \ \mathrm { i . e . }$ , to include robustness margins.

===== [p71-72] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.1 Introduction (14块) =====
--[p72]-- 4. an experimental validation on a multivariable Active Vibration Isolation System.
Smooth approximations provide a solution to deal with non-smoothness and nonconvexity at the same time, and are encountered in non-smooth convex (Beck and Teboulle, 2012; Nesterov, 2005) and non-convex optimization (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017; Xu, 2001) to accelerate convergence and increase parameter space exploration.
This chapter is organized as follows. In Section 3.2, the problem is formulated.
--[p71]-- In many practical applications, including wafer scanners (Heertjes et al., 2020) and chemical processes (Rivera et al., 2009), dealing with $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constraints on the input and output signals is key to guarantee safe experiments.
The complexity of OED depends on the constraints. Consequently the availability of algorithms heavily depends on the specific constraints that are imposed.
--[p71]-- (1991), wherein the $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ is iteratively approximated by the $l _ { p } { \mathrm { - n o r m } }$ . The method can be applied to MIMO systems, but becomes computationally intensive for systems with many inputs and outputs.
An alternative approach to achieve a low crest-factor is by the generation of binary signals that approximate a prescribed spectrum, since binary signals achieve maximal power for a given amplitude constraint (Tan and Godfrey, 2001).
--[p71]-- In Yeong and Torquato (1998), a simulated annealing approach is employed to globally solve this nonconvex problem. The method is computationally intensive. Recursive algorithms based on a receding horizon concept are presented in
Larsson et al. (2013); Rojas et al. (2007a). The algorithms are of lower computational complexity but are not defined for MIMO systems.
--[p72]-- The main contributions of this chapter are:
1. An algorithm for $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ minimization of multivariable signals that exploits an exponential smoothing function to overcome local minima,
2. an eficient and scalable computation of the gradient and Hessian matrix, including a quantification of computational complexity,
3. an embedding of the algorithm in an excitation design framework for optimal Frequency Response Function (FRF) identification of multivariable systems,
--[p72]-- In Section 3.3, the $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ minimization algorithm is presented. A fast and scalable computational approach is presented in Section 3.4. The algorithm is embedded in a two-step experiment design framework for non-parametric FRF identification in Section 3.5. The framework is confronted with an experimental setup in Section 3.6. Conclusions are drawn in Section 3.7.
--[p72]-- In Manchester (2010), excitation signals are computed from an $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constrained time-domain optimization problem, which typically leads to binary signals. The method involves solving a large scale semi-definite program, which becomes computationally intractable for large MIMO systems.
--[p71]-- For $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ signal constraints, such approach cannot be applied, since the signal peak amplitude depends not only on the spectral power, but also on the phase (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012). The relationship between the peak amplitude and the phase is highly nonlinear and non-smooth (Guillaume et al., 1991), which complicates optimal design procedures, especially for multiple inputs multiple outputs (MIMO) systems.
--[p71]-- In case of power constraints, OED problems can often be formulated as convex optimization programs, with the input spectrum as decision variable (Hildebrand and Gevers, 2002; Jansson and Hjalmarsson, 2005). Such problems can readily be solved by convex optimization techniques (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004).
--[p71]-- In Schroeder (1970), a phase selection law is presented that often yields a low crest-factor for scalar-valued signals. In Van der Ouderaa et al. (1988a); Van der Ouderaa et al. (1988b), a timefrequency domain swapping method is presented for joint crest-factor reduction of input and output signals. Extension of these methods to MIMO systems is complicated due to the heuristic nature of the algorithms. An optimizationbased approach to crest-factor minimization is presented in Guillaume et al.
--[p72]-- Notations: Operator $\odot$ denotes the element-wise product, and $\otimes$ denotes the Kronecker product. Operations $X = { \mathcal { F } } ( x )$ and $x = { \mathcal { F } } ^ { - 1 } ( X )$ denote the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) of x and the inverse DFT of X (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012), respectively. x denotes the complex conjugate of x.
3
--[p71]-- To mitigate the full complexity in solving $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constrained OED problems, various methods have been developed to solve the related problem of crestfactor minimization for signals with a prescribed power spectrum. The crestfactor is the ratio between signal peak amplitude and signal power (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012). Exact solutions to crest-factor minimization are not available, hence methods tend to be of heuristic nature.
--[p71]-- 3.1 Introduction
The computation of optimal excitation signals plays a central role in the design of experiments for system identification. Typically, the aim of optimal experiment design (OED) is to maximize the signal-to-noise ratio in view of a selected model quality criterion within the system its operating constraints (Goodwin, 1977; Ljung et al., 1987).
--[p72]-- Although many approaches for OED have been developed for a wide range of system identification problems, tractable methods for current applications with increasing complexity, such as many inputs and outputs, are not available. The aim of this chapter is to develop an eficient algorithm for $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constrained design of excitation signals with prescribed power spectrum, that is particularly suitable for large scale and multivariable experiment design problems.

===== [p143-144] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.6.1 Experiment setup (8块) =====
--[p143]-- When there are no missing samples, the classical LPM provides an efective approach to FRF identification from transient data, as illustrated in Evers et al. (2020). In this section, identification in the presence of missing samples is considered.
5.6.1.2 Experiment description: The system is excited with a periodic excitation signal u consisting of $P = 1 2$ periods with period length $N = 7 2 0$ The sample time $T _ { s } = 5 \mathrm { s }$ , leading to a period duration of 1 hour.
--[p143]-- 5.6.1 Experiment setup
5.6.1.1 System description: The experimental setup is depicted in Figure 5.13. It consists of two aluminum cylinders separated by a piece of polyoxymethylene (POM). The setup represents an industrial mixed-material thermo-dynamic system, wherein the aluminum parts reflect a thermal conductor and the POM a thermal resistance.
--[p144]-- In addition, for the purpose of error analysis, a reference model $G ^ { \mathrm { r e f } }$ is identified from the complete data $y$ using the classical LPM estimator described in Section 5.4.1. The resulting reference model is depicted in black in Figure 5.15 and Figure 5.16.
--[p143]-- These data $y ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ form the input to the estimators. The corresponding frequency-domain representation $Y$ and $Y ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ are shown in Figure 5.14(b). The global frequency-domain impact of the locally missing samples in the time-domain is evident.
5.6.1.3 Estimator selection: In the following, the performance of the TIE and TVE are analyzed for wavelet parameter values $p = 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 .$ .
--[p143]-- The spectral magnitudes of the excitation signal u are uniformly distributed over the EF lines $\bar { k } = \gamma k P$ with $k = 0 , \ldots , \lfloor N / \gamma \rfloor - 1$ , with sparsity parameter $\gamma = 2$ , recall Section 5.4.1. The spectral phase is selected from a uniform random distribution on [0, 2π). Since the heat input is intrinsically constrained to be positive, the input spectrum has a positive-valued contribution at the 0-frequency, resulting in an ofset in the heater input.
--[p143]-- The measured output response y is depicted in Figure 5.14(a). The signal contains a significant transient response, e.g., due to the heater ofset. To mimic the situation of a data record $y ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ with missing samples, a number of $n _ { \mathrm { m } } = 1 1 0 5$ out of $\bar { N } = 8 6 4 0$ samples is set to zero via the selection matrix M in (5.6). The missing samples form five data gaps, as shown in Figure 5.14(a).
--[p143]-- The system is equipped with a single heater and a single temperature sensor at the indicated locations, which form the system input u and output y, respectively. The system is operated in openloop.
The system dynamics are characterized by slow thermo-dynamic behavior, which gives rise to significant transient behavior with a duration of several hours.
--[p144]-- The polynomial order R in (5.14) for the transient estimation is selected as $R = 3$ , based on the LPM guidelines in Pintelon et al. (2010). For the TVE, a number of $n _ { t _ { c } } = 1 0$ basis functions $\varphi$ is selected, see (5.51), to emphasize on low-frequency accuracy in the computation of the time-varying wavelet functions $\tilde { \psi } .$

===== [p76-77] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.3.2 Smooth approximation function (6块) =====
--[p76]-- 3.3.2 Smooth approximation function
To handle the complexity introduced by both the non-smoothness and the nonconvexity in (3.8), the function $l _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \left( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) \right)$ is approximated by the smooth function,
$$
L ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = \sigma \ln \left( \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \bar { N } - 1 } \exp \left( \frac { \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( n ) } { \sigma } \right) \right) ,\tag{3.9}
$$
where $\bar { N } = n _ { y } N$ and $\sigma > 0$ .
--[p76]-- Properties of the function $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ are given in the following.
Lemma 3.3.2. Consider the function $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ for $\sigma > 0$ . Then,
i) $l _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } ( \bar { z } ) \le L ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) \le l _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } ( \bar { z } ) + \sigma \ln ( \bar { N } ) ,$
ii) $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { \sigma \to 0 } ~ L ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = l _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } ( \bar { z } ) } \end{array}$
--[p76]-- iii) $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ is increasing with respect to $\sigma ,$
iv) $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ is twice continuously diferentiable everywhere,
3
v) The gradient $\nabla _ { \Phi } L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ is expressed as
$$
\nabla _ { \Phi } L ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = \frac { \partial L } { \partial \phi } = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \bar { N } - 1 } \lambda _ { n } ( \Phi , \sigma ) \nabla _ { \Phi } \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( \Phi , n ) ,\tag{3.10}
$$
where
$$
--[p76]-- This function is based on the exponential penalty function presented in Bertsekas (1977); Kort and Bertsakas (1972) for solving convex programs with inequality constraints. It is also applied for smoothing of minmax problems in Xu (2001). Furthermore, (3.9) is a specific form of the logsum-exp function encountered in inference and classification in machine learning, $\mathrm { e . g . }$ , Calafiore et al. (2019).
--[p77]-- \lambda _ { n } ( \Phi , \sigma ) = \frac { \exp ( \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( n ) / \sigma ) } { \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \bar { N } - 1 } \exp ( \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( n ) / \sigma ) } , \quad \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \bar { N } - 1 } \lambda _ { n } = 1 .\tag{3.11}
$$
See Calafiore et al. (2019); Xu (2001) for a proof. Numerically stable implementations of $L$ that avoid overflow are addressed in Blanchard et al. (2021); Xu (2001). The function properties are demonstrated in the following example.
--[p77]-- Example 3.3.3. The result of the smoothing function (3.9) onto the signal in Example 3.3.1 is shown in Figure 3.4. The function $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ approaches the $l _ { \infty } ( \bar { z } )$ from above for decreasing values of σ.
The optimization problem based on L is formulated next.

--[p222]-- 7.7.3.3 RICR coprime factorizations: The RICR coprime factorizations of the plant models $\hat { P }$ and $\hat { P } ^ { \mathrm { F R F } }$ are computed using Theorem 7.5.4. 7.7.3.4 Uncertainty modeling: A norm-bounded uncertainty $\Delta$ in $( 7 . 7 )$ must be selected such that $P _ { o } \in \mathcal { P }$ .
--[p223]-- This observation is typical for noncollocated inferential control and is in line with the discussion in Section 7.3.1, where it is shown that the control performance depends on the specific dynamics of the entries in P. In contrast, in the classical robust motion control problem, low-frequency model uncertainties are typically dealt with by high-gain control, which relaxes the demand for accurate low-frequency modeling.
--[p221]-- 7.7.3 Robust inferential control design
7.7.3.1 Weighting filter selection: The weighting filters are selected using prior knowledge on the system dynamics and the characteristics of the desired loop shapes. The target bandwidth is selected as $f _ { b w } = 4 0$ , and the characteristic frequencies for low-frequency performance and high-frequency controller roll-of are selected as $f _ { r } = f _ { d } = 2 0 , f _ { c } = 2 0 0$
--[p222]-- The identified non-parametric uncertainty $\Delta ^ { \mathrm { F R F } }$ is shown in $( \times )$ in Figure 7.13. To ensure that the true dynamics are encompassed by the model set, the size of the uncertainty $\Delta$ is selected that upper-bound the true uncertainty, i.e.,
$$

--[p260]-- Curriculum Vitae
Nic Dirkx was born on July 10th, 1985 in Oss, the Netherlands. After finishing secondary education in 2003 at the Udens College in Uden, the Netherlands, he studied Mechanical Engineering at the Eindhoven University of Technology. During his studies, he performed an internship at the University of Melbourne, Melbourne, Australia on the topic of control design for haptic manipulators. He graduated within the Control Systems Technology group in 2011.
--[p260]-- His master thesis, entitled ‘Robot modeling and navigation systems design for a service robot’ was carried out under the supervision of Ren´e van de Molengraft and Janno Lunenburg.
In 2012, Nic joined the company Nobleo Technology, Eindhoven, the Netherlands, where he worked as research engineer at ASML, Veldhoven, the Netherlands in the field of design, dynamic modeling, and control for precision mechatronics.
--[p260]-- The results of the research are presented in this thesis and focus on experiment design, system identification, and control for complex high-end industrial applications.
--[p260]-- From 2018, Nic is senior researcher within ASML Research Mechatronics & Control, where his research is focused on identification and control of high-precision semiconductor equipment.
While working as senior researcher, Nic has pursued a Ph.D. degree under the financial support of ASML Research Mechatronics & Control, at the department of Mechanical Engineering at the Eindhoven University of Technology, under the supervision of Tom Oomen and Koen Tiels.

===== [p20-20] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.4.1 State-of-art in Identification of Precision Systems (6块) =====
--[p20]-- The main reason for the success of non-parametric FRF identification is that it provides a relatively simple approach to obtain thorough insight into the system dynamics, while requiring only mild assumptions on the actual system (Schoukens et al., 2009).
The accuracy of identified models, either parametric or non-parametric, is determined by the quality of the data that is obtained from the identification experiments (Goodwin, 1977).
--[p20]-- 1.4.1 State-of-art in Identification of Precision Systems
Frequency-domain modeling plays a central role in the design, the analysis, and control of precision equipment (Van de Wal et al., 2002; Voorhoeve et al., 2020a). Indeed, many important control engineering approaches, including loop shaping controller design, rely on Bode and Nyquist criteria that connect to frequencydomain system representations (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005).
--[p20]-- Frequency-domain identification provides the means to obtain such representations from measured data, and is generally considered as a fast, inexpensive, and accurate modeling approach compared to first principles modeling (Hjalmarsson, 2005). Besides parametric models required for model-based control, e.g., for inferential control discussed in Section 1.3.2, the use of non-parametric frequency response function (FRF) models is ubiquitous in many control applications.
--[p20]-- A necessary prerequisite for obtaining accurate models is a good signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), which may be achieved by using high-quality sensors and a suficiently exciting excitation signal. In traditional industrial practice, random (noise) excitation sequences are used, possibly with additional filtering, e.g., to mitigate aliasing efects (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012).
--[p20]-- In the face of the increasing complexity and tightening requirements for the envisioned next-generation systems, the traditional identification approaches do no longer lead to the desired performance. Hence, there is a need for eficient and accurate system identification techniques that can handle the considered complexities. The main research challenges investigated in this thesis are formulated in the next section.
--[p20]-- More recently, industrial practice is shifting toward the use of periodic excitation signals, e.g., multisines, to benefit from the advantages of determinism and periodicity (Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012). Typically, simple and ad-hoc approaches for the design of the excitation signals are pursued, e.g., based on engineering rules of thumb.

===== [p29-29] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.6.3 Contribution 3: Identification of model sets for high-performance robust control (2块) =====
--[p29]-- 1.6.3 Contribution 3: Identification of model sets for high-performance robust control
In Chapter 6, a framework is developed for optimal identification and experiment design to obtain model sets for high-performance robust control of complex motion systems. Hereby, Research Challenge R3 is addressed.
--[p29]-- The key approach is to connect experiment design, identification and robust control via a specific non-normalized, robust-control-relevant coprime factorization that is intimately related to non-conservative robust performance and robust stability metrics. The results constitute the following contribution.
Contribution C3. An optimal identification and experiment design framework for the identification of model sets for high-performance robust control of complex motion systems.

--[p163]-- 6.2.3 Objectives and contributions
To achieve high-performance robust control, it is crucial that the estimation of the model set and the identification experiment are performed in view of the control criterion. This is key in the formulation of the objectives.

===== [p33-34] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.1 Introduction (15块) =====
--[p33]-- Optimal Experiment Design (OED) consists in a systematic approach for the design of input signals that maximize the FRF accuracy within limited resources. The topic of OED has received much research interest in the last decades, see the overviews Gevers (2005); Hjalmarsson (2005); Mehra (1974a).
Dealing with system constraints is a necessary aspect in experiment design to achieve practical requirements such as plant-friendly operation (Deshpande and Rivera, 2014).
--[p33]-- (1987), Ch. 13; Goodwin (1977), Ch. 6; Bombois et al. (2011); Hjalmarsson (2005). Typically, the aim is to formulate the design problem as the minimization of a convex objective function over a convex constraint set, see e.g., Bombois et al. (2006); Gerencs´er et al. (2016); Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005); Lindqvist and Hjalmarsson (2000).
--[p34]-- 3. an excitation design approach based on a Relaxation and Randomization (RR) algorithm, including theoretical and practical performance bounds,
4. a comparison of model accuracy and time-eficiency between both algorithms based on experimental results from a next-generation wafer stage.
The organization of this chapter is as follows. In Section 2.2, the mechatronic system is introduced and the experiment design framework and objective is established.
--[p33]-- For many real-life mechatronic systems, however, constraints on the total power do not adequately represent the actual, often element-wise system limitations.
In experiment design for MIMO systems, addressing element-wise constraints in a non-conservative manner is crucial to maximize the model quality. In prior work Dirkx et al.
--[p33]-- 2.1 Introduction
High-precision positioning systems face ever increasing performance demands in terms of accuracy and productivity (Schmidt et al., 2020). These mechatronic systems, including wafer scanners, printing systems, and medical scanners, typically exhibit complex multivariable dynamics, e.g., due to flexible mechanics. Meeting the performance demands requires the employment of advanced modelbased control strategies (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005).
--[p33]-- Numerical optimization-based approaches that allow for multivariable design beyond orthogonal excitations are developed in Barenthin et al. (2008); Mehra (1974b). Herein, input design problems under total input or output power constraints are considered, which are typically convex problems and hence can be solved up to arbitrary precision (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004).
--[p34]-- (2019), it is shown that the design problem under element-wise constraints requires solving a non-convex optimization problem, but no general computational tools are provided. In related work Dirkx and Oomen (2020), preliminary results to solve this problem are reported.
Although many results on OED are present, fast and accurate identification of complex multivariable mechatronic systems is hampered by a lack of design approaches that can deal with element-wise constraints.
--[p33]-- The quality of the identified FRF depends on the experiment (Hjalmarsson, 2005) and therefore these experiments must be designed carefully. This applies especially to next-generation mechatronic systems, equipped with a large number of actuators (Oomen, 2018; van Herpen et al., 2014), since the number of experiments increases with the number of inputs.
--[p34]-- In Section 2.3, the role of multivariable excitations in identification of MIMO systems is illustrated. Section 2.4 presents diferent algorithms to solve the design problem. The experimental results are presented in Section 2.5, followed by the conclusions in Section 2.6.
--[p34]-- The aim of this chapter is to solve the OED problem for FRF identification of complex multivariable systems under element-wise power constraints.
The main contributions in this chapter are:
1. A framework for optimal multivariable excitation design for FRF identification of MIMO systems under element-wise power constraints,
2. a Sequential Semi-Definite Relaxation (SSDR) algorithm to approximately solve the multivariable excitation design problem, including feasibility and convergence results,
--[p33]-- For many mechatronic systems such as motion stages, the constraints are related to physical limitations of specific elements of the system, e.g., a bounded power for a specific actuator (Dirkx et al., 2019).
OED for Single Input Single Output (SISO) systems is well-developed, which has led to a large number of theoretical results and design frameworks. Design methods for parametric identification of SISO systems based on numerical optimization schemes are developed in, e.g., Ljung et al.
--[p33]-- In Pintelon and Schoukens (2012, Ch 2, 5), Dobrowiecki et al. (2006); Guillaume et al. (1996), the strategy for dealing with multiple inputs is to choose the excitation directions orthogonal to each other. These orthogonal excitations are (D-)optimal in the case of input power constraints, but not in the general case, e.g., under bounded input and output power.
--[p33]-- The identification of FRF models from experimental data, as opposed to first principles modeling, is considered fast, inexpensive, and accurate (Ljung et al., 1987; Pintelon and Schoukens, 2012).The resulting FRF constitutes an intermediate step towards parametric modeling and is also used directly for control design (Karimi and Kammer, 2017) and system diagnostics (Van der Maas et al., 2016).
--[p33]-- The majority of these control techniques rely on the availability of accurate FRF models (Oomen, 2018; Steinbuch and Norg, 1998; Van de Wal et al., 2002).
--[p33]-- Because the optimal experiment depends on the system itself, preliminary experiments may be employed to acquire the required problem data prior to the actual optimization (Hildebrand and Gevers, 2002).
For Multiple Inputs Multiple Outputs (MIMO) systems, the design problem becomes significantly more complex. For such systems, the problem involves not only the design of excitation magnitudes, but also the excitation directions (Dirkx et al., 2019).

===== [p176-176] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.4.6 Concluding remarks (3块) =====
--[p176]-- The size of this region is fundamentally limited by the uncertainty in the experimental data, which makes experiment design a crucial aspect in achieving high-performance robust control. --[p176]-- 6.4.6 Concluding remarks

$$

$$
--[p176]-- which jointly form the RCR model set $\mathcal { P } ^ { \mathrm { R C R } }$ in Definition 6.2.3. The obtained model set can readily be used for robust controller synthesis, as indicated in ( ) in Figure 6.3, e.g., using the standard techniques in Zhou et al. (1996).
The presented identification algorithm delivers a model that is near-optimal for robust control given the initial uncertainty region $\mathcal { D } .$ .

===== [p112-112] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> (3块) =====
--[p112]-- The framework encompasses both a time-invariant and a time-varying wavelet-based estimator, which provide diferent mechanisms to address the missing samples. Experimental results from a thermo-dynamical system confirm that the estimators enable accurate identification.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C2 of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. et al. (2023c). A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification From Incomplete Data.
IEEE Transactions on Instrumentation and Measurement, 72:1–15.
--[p112]-- The aim of this chapter is to achieve accurate identification of non-parametric FRF models of periodically excited systems from noisy output measurements with missing samples. An identification framework is established that exploits a wavelet-based transform to separate the efect of the missing samples in the time domain from the system characteristics in the frequency domain.
--[p112]-- A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: Frequency Response Function (FRF) estimation from measured data is an essential step in the design, the control, and the analysis of complex dynamical systems, including thermal and motion systems. Especially for systems that require long measurement time, missing samples in the data record, e.g., due to measurement interruptions, often occur.

--[p164]-- 6.3 Robust-control-relevant uncertainty structure
In this section, the uncertainty structure is selected that enables the construction of a model set that is RCR in the sense of Definition 6.2.3. This specific structure plays a key role in the identification approach in Section 6.4, the experiment design in Section 6.5, and their interrelations.
--[p164]-- The $\mathrm { L F T }$ interconnection (6.8) encompasses diferent common uncertainty structures such as (inverse) additive, multiplicative, or cf-based structures (Zhou et al., 1996). Achieving a RCR model set imposes specific requirements on the uncertainty structure selection.

===== [p86-86] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.4.3 Computational complexity (2块) =====
--[p86]-- For MIMO systems, the complexity of the gradient and Hessian computation additionally scales with $n _ { z } n _ { u }$ and $n _ { z } n _ { u } ^ { 2 }$ , respectively.
Evidently, by exploiting circular convolution theory, the fast approach eliminates the quadratic complexity in $N _ { k }$ in the gradient computation for both the direct approach (since $N N _ { k } > N _ { k } ^ { 2 } )$ and the $L _ { p }$ approach.
For the Hessian, the cubic complexity in $N _ { k }$ in the direct method is eliminated in the $L _ { p }$ and the fast method by employing the FFT.
--[p86]-- 3.4.3 Computational complexity
The complexity of the computation of the gradient and the Hessian is quantified in Table 3.1 for a SISO system. Herein, a comparison is performed between: direct computation from (3.20), the approach presented in Guillaume et al. (1991) for the $L _ { p } { \mathrm { - n o r m } }$ based method, and the fast approach by Theorems 3.4.1 and 3.4.2. The Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) implementation of the DFT is assumed.
