--[p28]-- This enables separating the efects of the missing samples in the time domain from the system and transient characteristics in the frequency domain. This leads to the following contribution.
Contribution C2. A wavelet-based method for non-parametric FRF identification from measured data with missing data parts.
--[p28]-- 1.6.2 Contribution 2: Identification from incomplete data
In Chapter 5, the problem of non-parametric FRF identification in the presence of missing data is investigated, as expressed in Research Challenge R2. An identification algorithm is developed that utilizes local estimation techniques similar to those in the LPM (Pintelon et al., 2010), in combination with a wavelet-based transform.

===== [p157-159] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.1 Introduction (19块) =====
--[p157]-- (1995), as well as identification and experiment design approaches aimed at minimization of the ν-gap metric (Bombois et al., 2000; Hildebrand and Gevers, 2002; Hjalmarsson, 2005; Jansson and Hjalmarsson, 2005).
An important step towards the identification of model sets for non-conservative control lies in the exclusion of candidate models that are invalidated by the experimental conditions.
--[p157]-- In particular, the use of the dual-Youla-Kuˇcera (dYK) structure (Anderson, 1998), which connects the plant coprime factors to those of the controller, is a vital step to exclude systems that are not stabilized by the controller that is used during the identification experiments, as shown in Anderson (1998); De Callafon and Van den Hof (1997); Douma and Van den Hof (2005).
--[p158]-- Even though the use of normalized coprime factors in identification is attractive due to its close connection with robust stability results, especially when paired with the dYK structure, it does not guarantee that the model set also leads to high performance control. The reasons are at least twofold. First, the (ν-) gap metrics that are inherently connected to normalized coprime factors are overly conservative, especially for lightly damped systems (Lanzon and Papageorgiou, 2009).
--[p158]-- A non-normalized coprime factor-based algorithm for the identification of RCR model sets from ellipsoidal uncertainty regions delivered by classical stochastic identification (Ljung et al., 1987).
2. An experiment design algorithm that is tailored to the specific identification algorithm in contribution 1.
3. An experimental study on an industrial wafer stage, which confirms that the presented approach enables improving robust control performance.
--[p157]-- Identification for robust control consists in the identification of such model sets from data, e.g., Bombois et al. (2006); Hjalmarsson (2005); Oomen and Bosgra (2012).
Achieving high-performance robust control necessitates that the interrelation between robust control and system identification is addressed non-conservatively in the identification of the model set.
--[p157]-- A crucial aspect herein is the interplay with experiment design, since the size of the model set is largely determined by the experimental conditions under which the identification is performed (Bombois et al., 2006). Clearly, obtaining a model set that is robust-control-relevant (RCR) involves multiple aspects:
1) the relation between data-based uncertainty sets and the properties of the resulting controllers,
2) the definition and computation of RCR model sets,
--[p157]-- 6.1 Introduction
The performance of model-based controllers hinges on the quality of the underlying model. In identification for control, the purpose of the model is to design a high-performance feedback controller. In Albertos et al. (2007); Schrama (1992); Zang et al. (1995), control-oriented models are obtained by expressing the identification criterion in view of the control application.
--[p159]-- Specifically, the first stage extends the analysis tools related to ellipsoidal regions in Bombois et al. (2000); Bombois et al. (2006); Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005) towards an algorithm for the optimal computation of a non-parametric descriptions of RCR coprime factors. The second stage extends the classical open-loop Sanathanan-Koerner (SK) iterations (Sanathanan and Koerner, 1963) to a closed-loop algorithm with closed-loop stability guarantees.
--[p159]-- Contribution 1 consists in a two-stage procedure for the $\scriptstyle { \mathcal { H } } _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m }$ (near)-optimal identification of an uncertainty set around non-normalized RCR coprime factors. The techniques in contribution 1 extend upon the theory on non-normalized coprime factorizations (Lanzon and Papageorgiou, 2009) and related identification frameworks (Boeren et al., 2018; Oomen and Bosgra, 2012) by an embedding in a stochastic identification setting.
--[p159]-- Contribution 2 consists in a convex excitation design algorithm that is explicitly connected to the identification algorithm in contribution 1 to maximize robust control performance. The techniques extend upon the design methods in Hjalmarsson (2005); Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005) by incorporation of worst-case robust performance and stability metrics derived from non-normalized coprime factorizations.
The outline of this chapter is as follows.
--[p158]-- Second, normalized coprime factorizations do not provide a straightforward connection to robust performance, which may lead to significant conservatism, as observed in De Callafon and Van den Hof (2001); Oomen and Bosgra (2012).
--[p158]-- The benefits of using nonnormalized coprime factors in identification for high-performance robust control are demonstrated in Boeren et al. (2018); Oomen and Bosgra (2012). Relations with classical identification and experiment design are thus far absent, and hence the aformentioned aspects 1) - 3) are only partially explored.
--[p157]-- 3) the formulation and design of RCR experiments,
see Gevers (2005) for more details.
Identification for robust control is typically aimed at determining models that optimize certain robust stability and performance metrics that express a distance between a nominal model and a perturbed model. For example, robust stability can be measured in the gap metric (El-Sakkary, 1985) and the ν-gap metric (Vinnicombe, 2001).
--[p157]-- These metrics have a close connection to the discrepancy between the normalized coprime factors (Vidyasagar, 1985) of two systems. The relevance of normalized coprime factorizations in control has led to the development of techniques for normalized coprime factor identification, e.g., in De Callafon and Van den Hof (1997); De Callafon et al. (1996); Douma and Van den Hof (2005); Van den Hof et al.
--[p157]-- When modeling errors are present, the algorithms may not converge (Hjalmarsson et al., 1995), and hence are lacking stability and performance guarantees (Gevers, 2005).
To provide performance guarantees in the presence of model uncertainty, robust control aims at the computation of a controller that achieves the best possible worst-case performance for a model set that includes the true system (Zhou et al., 1996).
--[p158]-- Although important progress is made in identification for robust control, at present the potential of non-normalized coprime factorizations in identification and experiment design is not fully explored and exploited.
--[p158]-- The aim of this chapter is to establish an optimal model set identification and experiment design framework for high-performance robust control, by extending and connecting results from non-normalized coprime factorizations to results in classical stochastic identification and experiment design.
The main contributions in this chapter are:
1.
--[p158]-- In Lanzon and Papageorgiou (2009), it is shown that the design freedom in nonnormalized coprime factors, obtained by relaxing the normalization condition, can be exploited to derive robust stability metrics with significantly reduced conservatism compared to the (ν-) gap metrics. In addition, it is shown in Oomen and Bosgra (2012) that a similar non-normalized coprime factorization transparently connects to robust performance metrics.
--[p159]-- In Section 6.2, the identification and experiment design problems are defined. In Section 6.3, the RCR coprime factorbased uncertainty structure is introduced. In Section 6.4, the identification method is presented, constituting contribution 1. In Section 6.5, the experiment design algorithm that constitutes contribution 2 is presented. In Section 6.6, an experimental study on a wafer stage system is provided, which constitutes contribution 3. Conclusions are drawn in Section 6.7.

===== [p176-176] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.5 Robust-control-relevant experiment design (2块) =====
--[p176]-- 6.5 Robust-control-relevant experiment design
The achievable robust control performance is limited by the size of the set ${ \mathcal { D } } ,$ which in turn reflects the uncertainty in the experimental data. Hence, a good design of the identification experiments, and in particular of the excitation signal w in Figure 6.1(b), is essential.
--[p176]-- In this section, an experiment design algorithm is presented for the computation of RCR excitations $w ^ { \mathrm { R C R } }$ that minimize the size of the model set, as formulated in Definition 6.2.4. A key aspect is that the algorithm is tailored to the identification algorithm in Section 6.4.
6

===== [p236-238] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 8.2 Recommendations (13块) =====
--[p236]-- 8.2 Recommendations
Based on the results and insights obtained from this thesis, the following recommendations for ongoing research are formulated.
Robust-control-relevant identification for MIMO systems: As shown in Chapter 6, accounting for the robust control goal is crucial in identification and the design of experiments to ensure that the identified model leads to a high-performance robust controller.
--[p236]-- For the evaluation of robust control criteria over ellipsoidal parameter uncertainty regions, the method presented in Chapter 6 makes extensive use of the property that the true system is assumed to have a single input. Consequently, this method cannot generally be extended to MIMO systems.
--[p236]-- To enable application of the RCR identification approach to MIMO systems, alternative methods for the evaluation of robust control criteria over ellipsoidal regions must be developed, e.g., by further developing the separation of graph framework as in Barenthin et al. (2008) towards a numerically reliable method that achieves low conservatism.
--[p238]-- Particular challenges that are foreseen include (computational) complexity management, dealing with multi-physical aspects, and the development of intuitive and non-conservative robust stability and performance criteria. The introduction of posterior couplings to leverage existing control loops as in Evers et al. (2019) may be a valuable intermediate step towards a full integral control solution.
<sub>blio</sub><sup>graph</sup>
--[p237]-- This applies especially when the position-dependency causes fast-changing dynamics, e.g., due to the envisaged aggressive motion profiles that are required to meet the future productivity demands.
Control of systems-of-systems: Lithography systems consist of many subsystems, or modules. The overall system performance is determined by the level of achieved coordination between these modules.
--[p237]-- Especially when the stage and wafer exhibit pronounced spatio-temporal behavior originating to mechanical flexibilities, the resulting dynamics between the inputs and the point of exposure become position-dependent. Similarly, position-dependency in the measured outputs occurs when the stage moves with respect to stationary sensors. Accurate and time-eficient identification techniques for position-dependent systems is an important extension of the LTIbased methods presented in this thesis.
--[p237]-- Hence, for (non-collocated) inferential control problems, system identification does not deliver the required model between all the inputs and outputs. To obtain models for inferential control, a diferent modeling approach must be pursued. Model updating approaches in which prior models, e.g., based on first principles modeling, are updated using measured data, may provide an outcome, see e.g., the recent survey in Mottershead et al. (2020).
--[p237]-- Robust inferential control of position-dependent systems: The dynamics of inferential motion controllers, and especially in the presence of noncollocated inputs, depend strongly on the flexible dynamics of the system, as shown in Section 7.3. As discussed above, the dynamics between actuator and sensor become position-dependent when the spatial deformations of the moving stage are measured using stationary sensors.
--[p238]-- To manage the complexity in these systems-of-systems, traditional control approaches aim at dividing the overall control problem into separate subproblems at module-level, yet at the expense of the overall system performance. For the envisaged future precision equipment, it is expected that integral systems-of-systems control solutions (Jamshidi, 2008) are required to achieve the performance requirements.
--[p237]-- Techniques may consist in extending the currently stationary identification and experiment design approaches towards approaches capable of identifying while (continuously) sampling the positiondependency, e.g, based on linear parameter varying (LPV) system parametrizations (T´oth, 2010; Voorhoeve et al., 2020b). Wavelet-based transformations, as explored in Chapter 5, may provide potential for non-parametric identification of position-dependent systems (Staszewski and Wallace, 2014).
--[p237]-- Similar mechanisms may introduce position-dependency between other input and output channels. Extension towards control techniques that can efectively and robustly cope with positiondependency, e.g., by exploiting LPV control methods (Hofmann and Werner, 2014), is an important step in the face of the increasing performance demands for future systems.
--[p237]-- Identification of systems with inaccessible inputs and outputs: System identification aims at obtained models of the system dynamics between the excited inputs and the measured outputs. In inferential control problems, as considered in Chapter 7, the performance variables cannot be measured. In addition, in the case of non-collocated disturbances, the disturbance input channels can typically not be excited in a controlled manner.
--[p237]-- Foreseen challenges in model updating in view of the envisioned future semiconductor equipment are dealing with and combining diferent types of measurement data, e.g., combining fast-sampled position or temperature data with static spatial wafer maps.
Eficient identification of position-dependent systems: Many motion systems are characterized by position-dependent dynamics. For example, in the wafer exposure process, the point of exposure traverses the wafer during exposure.

A particularly eficient approximation technique is developed that relies on a specific randomization procedure.

===== [p35-36] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 2.2.1 Over-actuated wafer stage system (5块) =====
--[p36]-- In this chapter, experiment design methods are developed that eficiently deal with the large number of inputs by exploiting the design freedom in the excitation signals within the physical constraints of the closed-loop system.
--[p35]-- 2.2.1 Over-actuated wafer stage system
Complex high-precision positioning systems are considered. Envisaging nextgeneration control systems, the focus is particularly on systems equipped with a large number of spatially distributed actuators and sensors (Oomen, 2018). One such a system is the next-generation wafer stage system in Figure 2.1, which is considered in this chapter.
To perform fast motion tasks and high accelerations, the stage is designed as a lightweight mechanical structure.
--[p35]-- This enables control of additional structural deformations, besides the conventional six rigid-body motion degrees of freedom (translations and rotations) (Van Herpen et al., 2014). Specifically, the stage is actuated by 8 Lorentz actuators and sensed by 7 encoders, according to the configuration in Figure 2.2. The stage operates in closed-loop position control using a digital PID-like controller that achieves a bandwidth of 50Hz, with a sampling frequency of 5kHz.
--[p35]-- Hence, it exhibits complex dynamic behavior, $\mathrm { e . g . } _ { \mathrm { } } . _ { }$ , due to mechanical flexibilities. To achieve nanometer accurate positioning performance, the stage is magnetically levitated using passive gravity compensators. Additionally, the stage is over-actuated and over-sensed, i.e., the number of actuators and sensors is larger than six.
--[p35]-- Due to electro-mechanical limitations, the maximum allowable signal power for each of the inputs and outputs is 0.25W and 0.30mW, respectively.
Identifying such large-dimensional complex MIMO systems in an efective and eficient manner imposes specific demands on experiment design strategies.

===== [p81-81] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.3.5 Avoiding local minima (6块) =====
--[p81]-- 3.3.5 Avoiding local minima
A key aspect of Algorithm 3.3 is that a gradual refinement of the approximation function reduces the sensitivity to local minima. Next result shows that certain local minima vanish when $\sigma \to \infty$
Theorem 3.3.5. Consider the parameter set $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { M } = \{ \Phi : \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \nabla _ { \Phi } \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( \Phi , n ) \neq } \end{array}$ 0}.
--[p81]-- 5 ( a )$ The percentage of solutions that attains the global minimum for diferent values of the initial smoothing parameter $\sigma ^ { 0 }$ is shown in Figure $\it 3 . 5 ( b )$ . Evidently, for small values $o f \sigma ^ { 0 }$ , locally optimal solutions are obtained, whereas choosing larger values $o f \sigma ^ { 0 }$ enables avoiding local minima entirely. This illustrates the strength of the gradual smoothing-based approach in Algorithm 3.3.
--[p81]-- Hence, by (3.10) the gradient becomes $\begin{array} { r } { \nabla _ { \Phi } L \bigl ( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma \bigr ) = 1 / \bar { N } \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \nabla _ { \Phi } \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( \Phi , n ) } \end{array}$ . Then, by definition of the set M, $\nabla _ { \Phi } L ( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma ) \neq 0$ for $\forall \Phi \in { \mathcal { M } }$ .
--[p81]-- Suppose that the set $\Phi ^ { * } ( \sigma ) = \{ \Phi \in \mathcal { M } : \nabla _ { \Phi } L ( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma ) = \ddot { 0 } \}$ for some $\sigma > 0$ is non-empty and includes local minimizers to $L ( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma )$ . Then, the set $\Phi ^ { * } ( \sigma )$ is empty for $\sigma \to \infty$
Proof. From (3.11) it follows that $\lambda _ { n } \to 1 / \bar { N }$ , ∀n for $\sigma \to \infty$ .

===== [p123-123] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.4 Wavelet-based LPM (2块) =====
--[p123]-- 5.4 Wavelet-based LPM
In this section, the wavelet-based estimation algorithms are presented for the accurate FRF identification from measurements with missing samples. The methods extend the existing frequency-domain LPM (Pintelon et al., 2010; Pintelon et al., 2011) to estimators in the time-frequency plane.
--[p123]-- First, a recapitulation of the classical LPM approach is provided in Section 5.4.1, which provides the mathematical notation and foundation for the extended estimator presented in Section 5.4.2. This extension encompasses both a time-invariant wavelet based estimator (TIE) and a time-varying wavelet based estimator (TVE), which are presented in Section 5.4.3 and Section 5.4.4, respectively. The two estimators are unified in a single framework as presented in Section 5.4.5.

--[p145]-- As global indicator of estimation performance, consider the frequency-averaged model error defined as $\begin{array} { r } { \varepsilon _ { X } ^ { [ p ] } = \frac { 1 } { \tilde { N } } \sum _ { \tilde { k } } | G ^ { \mathrm { r e f } } ( \tilde { k } ) - \hat { G } _ { X } ^ { [ p ] } ( \tilde { k } ) | } \end{array}$ , where $X \in \{ \mathrm { T I E } , \mathrm { T V E } \}$ These indicators are depicted in ${ \bigl ( } \equiv { \bigr ) }$ in Figure 5.17.
--[p145]-- This outcome depends on the (application-) specific balance between the level of stochastic and systematic contributions. In general, the TIE produces low-bias models, yet with a larger stochastic uncertainty. Such models may be preferred as an intermediate model towards a parametric model. The TVE, on the other hand, can provide more accurate models when the measurement noise is large, or when the number of missing samples is large.

5
--[p145]-- For the TVE, the noise contribution is lower overall and increases only slightly for increasing $p .$ Observing that the total model error decreases with increasing $p$ implies that the model error is dominated by systematic errors for small $p ,$ which are reduced for larger values of $p .$ These observations are in line with the results in Section 5.4.4.
Overall, the smallest model error is achieved by the TVE for $p = 4$ .

===== [p32-32] Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup> (4块) =====
--[p32]-- A multivariable excitation design framework is established that explicitly addresses the frequency-wise directionality of the system to be identified. The design problem involves solving a rank-constrained optimization problem, which is non-convex and NP-hard in most cases. Two algorithms to solving this problem approximately are presented that rely on a convex (semi-definite) relaxation of the original problem. Additionally, exact solutions for several special cases are presented.
--[p32]-- Frequency Response Function identification for multivariable motion control: Optimal experiment design with element-wise constraints. Mechatronics, 71:102440.
--[p32]-- Frequency Response Function Identification for Multivariable Motion Control: Optimal Experiment Design with Element-Wise Constraints<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: Frequency Response Function (FRF) models are essential in mechatronic systems, with applications ranging from system design and validation to controller design and diagnostics. The aim of this chapter is to optimally design experiments for FRF identification of multivariable motion systems subject to element-wise power constraints.
--[p32]-- The two algorithms are shown to overcome the limitations of traditional excitation design. This is confirmed by experimental results from a $7 \times 8$ wafer stage setup, which show a significant improvement of the FRF quality using the proposed techniques over traditional design approaches.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C1.A of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. et al. (2020).

===== [p78-79] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.3.3 Optimization problem formulation (4块) =====
--[p78]-- 3.3.3 Optimization problem formulation
The smoothing function $L ( \bar { z } , \sigma )$ in (3.9) is exploited to approximate the original non-smooth problem (3.8) by the smooth problem,
$$
\underset { \Phi } { \mathrm { a r g m i n } } \ L \left( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma \right) ,\tag{3.12}
$$
for some selected $\sigma > 0$ .
--[p78]-- To obtain a quadratic form that is compatible with Gauss-Newton-type (GN) algorithms (Wright, Nocedal, et al., 1999), the following equivalent problem to (3.12) is considered,
$$
\underset { \Phi } { \operatorname { a r g m i n } } \ \tilde { L } \left( \bar { z } ( \Phi ) , \sigma \right) ,\tag{3.13}
$$
where
$$
\tilde { L } ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \varepsilon ^ { T } ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) \varepsilon ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) ,\tag{3.14}
$$
with
$$
--[p79]-- \varepsilon ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = \left[ \exp \left( \frac { \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( 0 ) } { 2 \sigma } \right) , \dots , \exp \left( \frac { \bar { z } ^ { 2 } ( \bar { N } - 1 ) } { 2 \sigma } \right) \right] ^ { T } .\tag{3.15}
$$
The gradients of L and $\tilde { L }$ are equal up to a scale factor, since
$$
\nabla _ { \Phi } L ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) = \sigma \tilde { L } ^ { - 1 } ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) \nabla _ { \Phi } \tilde { L } ( \bar { z } , \sigma ) ,\tag{3.16}
$$
--[p79]-- and hence L and $\tilde { L }$ have identical minimizers.
Various optimization algorithms may be employed to solve (3.13). Typically, the parameters $\Phi$ are updated iteratively by an update law of the form
$$
\Phi ^ { i + 1 } = \Phi ^ { i } - ( \mathcal { H } ^ { i } ) ^ { - 1 } ( \mathcal { I } ^ { i } ) ,\tag{3.17}
$$
with i the iteration index, and where J and H contain curvature information of $\tilde { L } .$ . In the next section, an iterative algorithm is presented for solving (3.8).

--[p75]-- 3.3 A smoothing-based algorithm to ${ \mathbf { \omega } } _ { l _ { \infty } - \mathrm { n o r m } }$ minimization

===== [p218-218] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.6.1 Control goal (2块) =====
--[p218]-- c1) Conventional: only actuator $u _ { 1 }$ and sensor $y _ { 1 }$ in Figure 7.5 are used.
c2) Overactuation: actuators $u _ { 1 } , u _ { 2 }$ and sensor $y _ { 1 }$ are used.
c3) Overactuation & over-sensing: actuators $u _ { 1 } , u _ { 2 }$ and sensors $y _ { 1 } , y _ { 2 }$ are used.

Discrete-time linear time invariant (LTI) systems are considered. --[p159]-- Dual definitions apply to a left coprime factorization (lcf), where $\stackrel { \cdot } { P } = \tilde { D } ^ { - 1 } \tilde { N }$ denotes a lcf of $P .$ Furthermore, $\{ N _ { c } , D _ { c } \}$ and $\{ \tilde { N } _ { c } , \tilde { D } _ { c } \}$ denote a rcf and lcf of a controller $C .$ , respectively.
--[p159]-- The pair $\{ N , D \}$ is a normalized rcf (nrcf) of $P$ if $\{ N , D \}$ is a rcf and $D ^ { * } D + N ^ { * } N = I$ , where $D ^ { * } ( e ^ { j \omega } ) = D ( e ^ { - j \omega } ) ^ { T }$ . A normalized coprime factorization will be indicated by the accent¯.

--[p214]-- 7.5.1 RICR model set criterion

$$
--[p214]-- In the following, it is assumed that $\hat { P }$ is available, e.g., from first principles modeling or robust-control-relevant system identification (Oomen and Bosgra, 2012). ===== [p214-214] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.5 Constructing model sets for robust inferential control (2块) =====
--[p214]-- In this section, a method is presented for the construction of such RICR model sets for systems with unmeasurable performance variables and non-collocated disturbances.
--[p214]-- 7.5 Constructing model sets for robust inferential control
The achievable robust control performance in terms of (7.9) is crucially afected by the size and shape of the model set $\mathcal { P } .$ . This motivates the construction of a model set that is robust-inferential-control-relevant (RICR), i.e., a set that has minimal dimension in view of the control goal (7.9).

===== [p219-219] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.7.1 Control goal (2块) =====
--[p219]-- 7.7.1 Control goal
The wafer stage setup in the configuration as shown in Figure 7.1 is considered. The control goal is to position the point $z _ { p }$ by means of a robust inferential controller that only has access to the measurements $y _ { p }$ . The point $z _ { p }$ is considered unmeasurable during operation but it is monitored for the purpose of performance evaluation. The disturbance $d _ { p }$ is mimicked by an actuator at the indicated location.
--[p219]-- The actuators used for control purposes are indicated by $u _ { p }$ Input decoupling (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005) is applied to transform the four actuator forces into a single vertical force. Similarly, output decoupling is applied to transform the three measured displacements at the sensors to a single vertical displacement measurement. Consequently, the entries in P reduce to SISO systems.
The results in this section focus on a SISO controller element for the control of vertical displacements at the point $z _ { p }$

===== [p26-27] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.5 Advanced Model-based Control (5块) =====
--[p26]-- 1.5 Advanced Model-based Control
The availability of accurate identified models of the considered complex systems is an enabler for the success of the envisioned advanced model-based control strategies. In this thesis, strategies for the non-collocated inferential control problem are investigated.
--[p27]-- Develop a systematic controller design approach for high-performance robust control of complex MIMO motion systems with unmeasured performance variables and non-collocated disturbances.
--[p26]-- Research Challenge R4: Non-collocated inferential control: The presence of non-collocated inputs and outputs in inferential control significantly affects the required structure and dynamics of the controller. In Oomen et al. (2014), it is shown that for inferential motion control, generally a two DOF controller structure is required to position the point of interest , as opposed to the traditional single DOF controller structure.
--[p26]-- In addition, in Hong and Bernstein (1998), it is shown that the presence of non-collocated inputs gives rise to both diferent requirements on the controller dynamics, and to diferent control performance limitations, as compared to the traditional control problem.
--[p26]-- Achieving the performance demands for the envisioned future systems necessitates the development of control approaches that explicitly deal with the distinct properties and requirements of the non-collocated inferential control problem. Particular aspects include the establishment of design rules, the use of over-actuation and over-sensing strategies, and the development of model uncer-
1
Research Challenge R4.

===== [p96-96] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.2.1 Complex mechatronic systems (2块) =====
--[p96]-- Achieving accurate system identification demands experiment design techniques that can handle such complex MIMO systems. In this chapter, a robust experiment design approach for such systems is presented.
--[p96]-- 4.2.1 Complex mechatronic systems
Complex MIMO mechatronic systems are considered, such as the nextgeneration wafer stage system in Figure 4.1. To perform fast motion tasks, the stage is designed to be lightweight. As a consequence, it exhibits complex dynamic behavior, e.g., due to mechanical flexibilities. To achieve extreme positioning performance, the stage is equipped with 8 actuators and 7 sensors, enabling explicit control of internal flexibilities (Van Herpen et al., 2014).

===== [p167-167] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.4 Robust-control-relevant model set identification (1块) =====

===== [p147-147] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.7 Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p147]-- Contrary to most existing algorithms for dealing with missing samples, the presented approach does not require the explicit estimation or reconstruction of the missing data parts. Simulation studies and a confrontation with a thermo-dynamical experimental setup confirm that accurate FRF identification is achieved.
--[p147]-- 5.7 Conclusions
The presented methods achieve accurate identification of non-parametric FRF models from measured data records with many missing samples. This is realized by generalizing the existing frequency-domain LPM to a wavelet-based LPM that enables isolating the efect of the missing samples in the time-frequency plane. The generalization encompasses a time-invariant estimator and a time-varying estimator, which have diferent performance in terms of bias and variance errors.

--[p225]-- 7.7.4.3 Time-domain results: The wafer stage is simultaneously subjected to a trapezoidal disturbance profile $d _ { p } ( t ) \ ( - - )$ and a sinusoidal reference trajectory $r _ { z } ( t ) \ ( - )$ . The achieved inferential control errors $e _ { z } ( t )$ for $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } }$ and $\dot { C } ^ { \mathrm { c l a s } }$ are shown in $( - )$ and $( - )$ , respectively, in Figure 7.15.
--[p225]-- Clearly, the inferential controller $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } }$ eliminates the error contribution resulting from a static disturbance $d _ { p }$ , whereas the error is constant during the rising and falling edges of the disturbance profile. This is in sharp contrast to the classical controller $C ^ { \mathrm { c l a s } }$ ， which is incapable of eliminating the static error, and shows an error increase that is proportional to the time derivative of the disturbance signal.
--[p225]-- Overall, the results support the theoretical statements in this chapter and verify that the developed techniques enables improving performance at the unmeasured point of interest compared to the classical control approach.
7
--[p224]-- 7.7.4.2 Frequency-domain results: The disturbance suppression characteristics expressed by $T _ { e _ { z } d } : d _ { p } \mapsto e _ { z }$ are shown in Figure 7.14 for $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } } \left( - \right)$ and $C ^ { \mathrm { c l a s } } \ ( - )$ The inferential controller achieves $\mathrm { ~ a ~ } { + 1 }$ slope for frequencies below 30Hz, while the slope of the classical controller rolls of to a non-zero ofset value below 0.3Hz.
--[p224]-- 7.7.4 Performance
7.7.4.1 Objective value: The nominal inferential controller achieves a nominal performance of $\mathcal { I } ( \hat { P } , C ^ { \mathrm { i n i t } } ) = 3 . 0$ and a robust performance of $\mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( \mathcal { P } ^ { \mathrm { R I C R } } , C ^ { \mathrm { i n i t } } ) ~ = ~ 5 . 9$ .
--[p224]-- Furthermore, $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } }$ achieves a consistent improvement below 30Hz compared to $C ^ { \mathrm { c l a s } }$ . This illustrates the incapability of the classical controller to deal with non-collocated disturbances at low frequencies, which supports the discussion in Section 7.3.
7
--[p224]-- These values verify that the performance bound (7.45) is tightly satisfied for the RICR uncertainty description norm-bounded by $\gamma _ { \mathrm { m a x } } = 2 . 9$ . The robust controller achieves $\mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { W C } } ( \mathcal { P } _ { \gamma } ^ { \mathrm { R I C R } } , C ^ { \mathrm { R P } } ) = 3 . 9$ , which is close to the nominal performance, indicating that the model set $\mathcal { P } ^ { \mathrm { R I C R } }$ is indeed RICR.

===== [p141-141] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.5.1 Simulation setup (3块) =====
--[p141]-- 5.5.1 Simulation setup
The system $G _ { o }$ is constituted by a $2 \times 2$ simulated LTI system with 2 highly damped modes, and 2 lightly damped modes. The system represents a damped flexible mechanical structure. Its FRF model is shown in ( ) in Figure 5.11. The system is closed-loop controlled by a PID-type feedback controller.
--[p141]-- The excitation signals are random phase orthogonal periodic excitations (Dobrowiecki et al., 2006) with uniform spectral magnitude distribution and $P = 5$ $N ~ = ~ 2 0 0 0 , ~ \gamma ~ = ~ 2$ The outputs z are measured with a sampling time of $T _ { s } ~ = ~ 0 . 0 2 \mathrm { s }$ , and a total number of 500 samples are missing per experiment. The outputs z are perturbed by white noise $\nu _ { y }$ .
--[p141]-- Due to the highly damped modes, a significant transient is present in the output z over the duration of the
experiments.
The TIE estimator is evaluated for the values $p = 1 , 2 , 3$ . For comparison, also the classical LPM is evaluated. The LPM polynomial order is set as $R = 2$ for both estimators.

===== [p16-17] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.3.1 State-of-art in Precision Control (6块) =====
--[p16]-- This property has significantly simplified the task of controller design, since it enables accurately decoupling the MIMO system into multiple SISO sub-systems in the frequency range that is typically relevant for control (Van de Wal et al., 2002). Subsequently, this allows for the application of standard SISO control design techniques, including well-known loop shaping procedures based on Bode and Nyquist diagrams (Skogestad and Postlethwaite, 2005).
--[p16]-- 1.3.1 State-of-art in Precision Control
In line with the traditional design philopshy, modern precision equipment in the semiconductor industry is characterized by highly stif and reproducible mechanical design. As a result, the considered mechanical structures behave predominantly as a rigid body over a large frequency range.
--[p17]-- Second, stifness increase may require an increase in moving mass, which poses a disadvantage for performing high-speed motion tasks. Third, neglecting internal deformation behavior, which is inherent to the rigid-body control approach, is no longer justified in view of the extreme precision requirements. Consequently, there is a need for control strategies that can efectively deal with the complexities in future precision systems. In the next section, the key control challenges are outlined.
--[p16]-- Examples include automated controller design procedures for bandwidth optimization (Bruijnen et al., 2006) and control strategies that exploit additional sensors and actuators to improve the rigid-body approximation (Silvas et al., 2011).
1
--[p16]-- The control performance of stif structures is mainly determined by the control bandwidth, i.e., the frequency range in which the controller is efective. The achievable bandwidth is typically limited by the resonance frequencies of the flexible mechanics. Traditional system design has therefore aimed at maximizing stifness to shift the resonance phenomena to higher frequencies. In parallel, several control strategies have been developed that aim at bandwidth increase.
--[p17]-- For the envisioned future precision systems, the pursuance of high-bandwidth rigid-body control strategies in conjunction with ever-stifer mechanics is expected to no longer be a viable and cost-efective approach to meet the performance demands (Oomen, 2018). A first reason is that increasing stifness is typically costly, e.g., due to the need of expensive materials.

===== [p92-92] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.7 Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p92]-- Additionally, it is shown that the smoothing-based algorithm is highly suitable for incorporation in a two-stage experiment design framework for the accurate identification of MIMO systems.
3
--[p92]-- Low computational complexity is achieved by exploiting structural properties in the gradient and Hessian matrix and results from circular convolution theory. Experimental results show a drastic improvement in both the achieved $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ and the computation time compared to existing techniques.
--[p92]-- 3.7 Conclusions
The presented method enables designing $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constrained excitation signals to improve the quality of identified models of large MIMO systems. This is realized by an optimization-based approach that exploits a sequence of smooth approximations of the non-smooth objective function to achieve a high level of robustness against local minima.

===== [p117-118] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.3.1 Wavelet-based transformation (6块) =====
--[p118]-- \xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } } [ n ] : = \{ z ^ { \mathrm { m } } \circledast \psi _ { j } \} [ n ] : = \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { \bar { N } } } \sum _ { m = 0 } ^ { \bar { N } - 1 } \psi _ { j } [ m ] \cdot z _ { \bar { N } } ^ { \mathrm { m } } [ n - m ] .\tag{5.8}
$$
By the convolution theorem, the transform $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ in (5.8) is equivalently expressed as the frequency-domain operation
$$
--[p118]-- \xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } } = ( Z ^ { \mathrm { m } } \odot \Psi _ { j } ) \mathcal { H } ,\tag{5.9}
$$
where $\Psi _ { j } \in \mathbb { C } ^ { 1 \times \bar { N } }$ is the DFT of $\psi _ { j }$ .
Expression (5.8) shows that $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ describes the characteristics of $z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ in the time domain, whereas (5.9) shows that $\Psi _ { j }$ acts as a frequency-domain windowing function upon $Z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ .
--[p117]-- 5.3.1 Wavelet-based transformation
The key step in separating the perturbation from the missing samples from the FRF of $G _ { z }$ in (5.4) is the transformation of the data $z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ to the time-frequency plane. This is realized via convolution with a set of short-time oscillations at a j-th frequency line, referred to as the wavelet functions $\psi _ { j }$
5
Definition 5.3.1 (Wavelet convolution).
--[p118]-- The circular convolution $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } } \in \mathbb { C } ^ { 1 \times \bar { N } }$ of the signal $z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ with the wavelet $\psi _ { j }$ at sample $n = [ 0 , \ldots , \bar { N } - 1 ]$ is defined as
$$
--[p118]-- Hence, $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ reflects the time-domain behavior of the frequency components of $Z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ within the selected window, i.e., $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ provides both time- and frequency-localized information of $Z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$
The transform $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ is afected by the missing samples in $z ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ .
The key idea is to confine this efect in $\xi _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ by selecting a suitable wavelet function, which is considered next.
--[p118]-- Consider a wavelet function $\psi _ { j } ~ \in$ $\mathbb { C } ^ { 1 \times { \bar { N } } }$ with center-frequency $j$ . Let $z _ { \bar { N } } ^ { \mathrm { m } }$ be the N<sup>¯</sup> -periodic extension of signal $z ^ { \mathrm { m } } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 1 \times \bar { N } }$ .

===== [p70-70] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> (4块) =====
--[p70]-- A Fast Smoothing-Based Algorithm to Generate l -Norm Constrained Signals for Multivariable Experiment Design. IEEE Control Systems Letters, 6:1784–1789 and Dirkx, N. et al. (2022a). Peak amplitude-constrained experiment design for FRF identification of MIMO motion systems. In 2022 IEEE 17th International Conference on Advanced Motion Control (AMC). IEEE, pages 256–261.
--[p70]-- Experimental results show excellent convergence behavior that overcomes local minima, while significantly reducing computation time compared to existing techniques. In addition, it is shown that the algorithm enables improving the quality of identified models when incorporated in an experiment design framework for multivariable systems.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C1.B of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. et al. (2021a).
--[p70]-- A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: Handling peak amplitude constraints, or equivalently $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m \ c o n - }$ straints, is an important application demand in experiment design for system identification.
--[p70]-- The aim of this chapter is to present a method for the design of excitation signals with a prescribed power spectrum under $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ constraints, for systems with many inputs and outputs. The method exploits an exponential smoothing function in an iterative algorithm. Fast convergence is achieved by a computationally eficient construction of the gradient and the Hessian matrix.

===== [p186-186] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.7 Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p186]-- The identification problem is aimed directly at determining the optimal coprime factors within an uncertainty region delivered by classical stochastic identification. It is shown that these optimal coprime factors are at the Chebyshev center in a robust-control-relevant uncertainty region. A two-stage algorithm is presented that estimates these coprime factors almost optimally.
--[p186]-- 6.7 Conclusions
Model sets for achieving high-performance robust control can be obtained from the identification and experiment design framework presented in this chapter. This is realized by exploiting a specific non-normalized, robust-control-relevant coprime factorization that transparently connects to distance measures relating to both robust performance and robust stability.
--[p186]-- In addition, an experiment design algorithm is presented that is explicitly tailored to the coprime factor identification algorithm. Experimental results from a prototype wafer stage confirm that both the identification and experiment design approach enable achieving a lower control
cost.

--[p12]-- The growth in computing power as a result of the ongoing miniaturization in semiconductors has enabled technologies that were unimaginable only a few years ago. The concurrent inclusion of mechanical, electrical, and control engineering with computer and data science in product design is referred to as mechatronic system design (Bolton, 2008).
--[p12]-- As long as Moore’s Law is maintained, miniaturization will keep on shaping the future world in ways that presently seem science fiction.

--[p13]-- Wafer scanners form the highly sophisticated machines that are responsible for the critical photolithography step in the chipmaking process (Butler, 2011). ===== [p100-101] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.3.1 Main idea: Iterative robust design via the S-lemma (5块) =====
--[p100]-- \begin{array} { r l r } & { \underset { \tilde { \Phi } , \gamma \succeq 0 } { \mathrm { m i n i m i z e ~ } } \gamma } & \\ & { \mathrm { s u b j e c t ~ t o ~ } \tilde { f } ( q _ { m } , \varepsilon , \tilde { \Phi } , \gamma ) \ge 0 \quad \forall m , \quad \varepsilon \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } } \\ & { } & { \tilde { g } ( \varepsilon , \tilde { \Phi } , \gamma ) \quad \ge 0 , \qquad \varepsilon \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } } , } \end{array}\tag{4.15}
$$
--[p100]-- that are solved exactly at each iteration. Here, the functions $\tilde { f } , \tilde { g }$ are quadratic approximations to the constraints in (4.9).
The key existing technical result that is employed to solve (4.15) is the S-lemma (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004), which provides conditions under which the non-negativity of one quadratic function is a consequence of another one.
4

$$
--[p101]-- f ( x ) = \left[ { \begin{array} { l } { x } \\ { 1 } \end{array} } \right] ^ { T } \left[ A _ { 1 1 } \quad A _ { 1 2 } \right] \left[ { \boldsymbol { x } } \right] ,\tag{4.16}
$$
for some $A _ { 1 1 } = A _ { 1 1 } ^ { H } \in \mathbb { C } ^ { n \times n } , A _ { 1 2 } \in \mathbb { C } ^ { n \times 1 } , A _ { 2 2 } \in \mathbb { R } .$
--[p101]-- Since, by virtue of (4.8), the constraint $\varepsilon \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { i n i t } }$ in (4.15) is quadratic, the following result is due to the S-lemma.

===== [p110-110] Iterative Robust Experiment Design for MIMO System Identification via the S-lemma<sup>1</sup> > 4.5 Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p110]-- The method avoids the discretization of the infinite-dimensional uncertainty set, as well as the inclusion of a large number of constraints in the optimization program, both of which are encountered in existing approaches such as the scenario approach. Experimental confrontation with a flexible beam setup shows that the method enables a significant robustification, and achieves a performance close to that of the scenario approach.
4
--[p110]-- 4.5 Conclusions
The method presented in this chapter enables input spectrum design for identification of complex MIMO systems that is robust to uncertainty in the nominal or initial plant model, for uncertainty sets in the form of ellipsoidal regions. The algorithm consists in an iterative procedure that alternates beween solving a convex approximate program and an update of the approximation functions.

--[p202]-- Requirements 1.
R1 The structure of the controller C must be suficiently rich to enable joint disturbance suppression and setpoint tracking.

===== [p88-89] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.5.2 Embedding the ${ \boldsymbol { l } } _ { \infty }$ -norm optimization step (5块) =====
--[p88]-- 3.5.2 Embedding the ${ \boldsymbol { l } } _ { \infty }$ -norm optimization step
For MIMO systems, the optimal excitations to (3.36), say $\tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] }$ , are multivariable, i.e., they have a frequency-wise directionality that depends on the directionality of the system (Dirkx et al., 2020). These directions are determined by both the excitation magnitudes and the phases. The latter implies that the phases, at least to a certain degree, are predetermined in the solution to (3.36).
--[p88]-- To guarantee that the performance achieved by $\tilde { W }$ in SP1 is maintained in the subsequent $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ optimization step in SP2, the phase design in SP2 must be such that it preserves the predetermined directions. This is achieved by exploiting the non-uniqueness in the solution to (3.36), i.e.,
$$
--[p89]-- \tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) \tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) = \tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] } ( k ) e ^ { j \phi _ { k } ^ { [ e ] } } ( e ^ { j \phi _ { k } ^ { [ e ] } } ) ^ { H } \tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] H } ( k ) .\tag{3.38}
$$
$\mathrm { S o } .$ in SP2, the remaining design freedom per experiment is in the selection of the frequency-wise phase parameter $\phi _ { k } ^ { [ e ] }$ .
--[p89]-- Under preservation of the excitation directions of $\tilde { W }$ , the outputs of the MIMO system G in (3.1) are given by
$$
z _ { p } ^ { [ e ] } ( n ) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N _ { k } } | M _ { p k } ^ { [ e ] } | \cos \left( \frac { 2 \pi k n } { N } + \phi _ { k } ^ { [ e ] } + \angle M _ { p k } ^ { [ e ] } \right) ,\tag{3.39}
$$
where $M _ { p k } ^ { [ e ] } = G _ { p k } \tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] } ( k )$ is a mapping in which the solution $\tilde { W } ^ { [ e ] }$ to SP1 is absorbed.
--[p89]-- Algorithm 3.3 is readily applicable to solve the associated $l _ { \infty }$ -norm minimization problem.
The resulting two-step OED framework is experimentally validated in the next section.

--[p207]-- 7.3.3 Improving performance by over-sensing and over-actuation
The presented analysis framework facilitates making control system design choices on sensor and actuator placement. The mitigation of the fundamental performance limitations by means of over-sensing and over-actuation is investigated in the following example.
Example 7.3.6. Consider the wafer stage setup described in Section 7.2.1.
--[p207]-- The control goal is to position the three unmeasured performance variables $z _ { 1 } , z _ { 2 } , z _ { 3 }$ , which represent vertical displacement at the three corners. Three spatially distributed disturbances $d _ { 1 } , d _ { 2 } , d _ { 3 }$ are considered.
Consider models $\hat { P } _ { 1 } , \hat { P } _ { 2 } , \hat { P } _ { 3 }$ corresponding the following sensor and actuator configurations. $\hat { P } _ { 1 }$ employs sensor $y _ { 1 }$ and actuator $u _ { 1 }$ only.
--[p208]-- System ${ \hat { P } } _ { 3 } \ \left( - \ - \right)$ also exploits an additional sensor, leading to a substantial reduction of $\bar { \sigma } ( T _ { \mathrm { s e n s } } ^ { \mathrm { m s } } )$ (middle), and consequently a further reduction of the total error $\bar { \sigma } ( T _ { e _ { z } d } ^ { \mathrm { m s } } ) ~ ( l e f t )$
--[p207]-- In the configuration considered in this simulation example, the stage has a single rigid-body motion DOF in the vertical direction, i.e., $n _ { \mathrm { r b } } = 1$ , and is equipped with two actuators $u _ { 1 } , u _ { 2 }$ and two sensors $y _ { 1 } , y _ { 2 }$ in the vertical direction, as indicated in Figure 7.5. Hence, this configuration allows for over-actuation $( n _ { u } > n _ { \mathrm { r b } } )$ and over-sensing $( n _ { y } > n _ { \mathrm { r b } } )$ control strategies.

===== [p156-156] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> (3块) =====
--[p156]-- This is achieved by connecting the criteria for robust control, identification, and experiment design through a non-normalized, robust-control-relevant (RCR) coprime factorization. Specifically, the identification algorithm entails a two-stage approach for the near-optimal computation of RCR coprime factors within ellipsoidal uncertainty regions delivered by classical stochastic identification.
--[p156]-- In addition, a RCR coprime factor-based experiment design algorithm is formulated that tightly connects to the presented identification algorithm. Experimental results from a prototype wafer stage demonstrate that the presented techniques enable improving robust control performance.
<sup>1</sup>The results in this chapter constitute Contribution C3 of this thesis. The chapter is based on Dirkx, N. and Oomen, T. (2023).
Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach. In preparation for journal submission.
--[p156]-- Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup>
Abstract: The achievable performance of robust controllers crucially depends on the model set. The aim of this chapter is to establish an identification and experiment design framework for the identification of model sets that enable high-performance robust control.

===== [p117-117] A Wavelet-Based Approach to FRF Identification from Incomplete Data<sup>1</sup> > 5.2.2 Key idea: estimation in the time-frequency plane (3块) =====
--[p117]-- Contrary to the DFT, the wavelet-based transform provides frequency information that is time-localized, allowing data to be represented in the two-dimensional time-frequency plane. Exploiting the extra dimension, the efect of the missing samples can be localized in the time-dimension, and separated from the system response in the frequency-dimension.
--[p117]-- 5.2.2 Key idea: estimation in the time-frequency plane
The main limitation in traditional FRF identification techniques originates from the fact that the DFT is a global transform, making it unsuitable for analysis of signals that exhibit local time-domain phenomena such as missing samples.
The key idea of the identification method presented in this chapter is to employ a local wavelet-based transform (Daubechies, 1990) rather than the global DFT transform.
--[p117]-- In the next section, the wavelet-based transform to the time-frequency plane is introduced, which is exploited for the formulation of wavelet-based estimators in Section 5.4.

===== [p208-208] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.4 H<sub>∞</sub>-norm optimal inferential control design framework (2块) =====
--[p208]-- 7.4 H<sub>∞</sub>-norm optimal inferential control design framework
The $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty }$ -norm optimal control framework has been successful in the control of high-precision motion systems (Oomen et al., 2014; Van de Wal et al., 2002), among other due to its natural connection to loop-shaping (Vinnicombe, 2001). This section presents the $\mathcal { H } _ { \infty }$ -norm optimal inferential controller design framework for nominal performance.
--[p208]-- The framework includes a multivariable loopshaping procedure that connects to the analysis framework in Section 7.3 by accounting for the potential performance limitations in non-collocated systems.

===== [p74-75] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.2.3 Objectives (5块) =====
--[p75]-- b) the $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ is a non-smooth and not everywhere diferentiable function, which prohibits employing standard optimization techniques,
c) the number of design variables in A, Φ is typically large, especially for large MIMO systems,
d) For MIMO systems, problem (3.3) consists in the design of both the excitation magnitudes and the directions, the latter of which are not present for SISO systems (Dirkx et al., 2020).
--[p75]-- Consequently, a dedicated and eficient strategy is required to solve (3.3). To obtain a tractable computational method to solve (3.3), the problem is separated into two subproblems (SP):
SP1: Computation of spectral magnitudes A that minimize $\mathcal { T } ,$ subject to powerconstraints,
SP2: $l _ { \infty } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ minimization over the variables Φ, for the predetermined spectral magnitudes A.
--[p75]-- The secondary goal is to embed the algorithm in a multivariable and directional OED framework for FRF identification, hereby addressing item d). This is presented in Section 3.5.
--[p74]-- 3.2.3 Objectives
The objective in this chapter is to present a tractable approach for excitation design that satisfies (3.3). Solving the OED problem (3.3) is a complex problem in general, both from a fundamental and computational perspective, especially for MIMO systems. The complexity is due to:
a) the constraint function $l _ { \infty } ( z _ { p } )$ is non-convex in the variables Φ, hence local minima should be dealt with,
--[p75]-- Such separation is common, as evidenced by, e.g., Hjalmarsson (2005); Jansson and Hjalmarsson (2005) that aim at solving SP1, whereas Guillaume et al. (1991); Van der Ouderaa et al. (1988b) address SP2.
In this chapter, the primary goal is to eficiently solve SP2 for large MIMO systems. In Sections 3.3 and 3.4, an algorithm is presented that explicitly addresses items a) - c).

===== [p180-180] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.6.2 Control performance specifications (2块) =====
--[p180]-- 6.6.2 Control performance specifications
The shape of the RCR model set depends on the controller specifications of the to-be-synthesized controller $C ^ { \mathrm { R P } }$ . The control performance specifications are reflected in the weighting filters $W _ { 1 } , W _ { 2 }$ in (6.4).
--[p180]-- To specify integral action and controller roll-of, the filter $W _ { 1 }$ is selected as a first order high-pass filter with a corner frequency of $\scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } f _ { \mathrm { B W } }$ , and $W _ { 2 }$ is selected as a low-pass filter with a corner frequency of $3 f _ { \mathrm { B W } } \mathrm { \bar { H z } }$ , respectively. Appropriate scaling is applied that targets
at a control bandwidth of f<sub>BW</sub>.

===== [p180-180] Identification and Experiment Design for Robust Control: A Non-Normalized Coprime Factor Approach<sup>1</sup> > 6.6.1 Wafer stage system (2块) =====
--[p180]-- 6.6.1 Wafer stage system
Wafer scanners are state-of-the-art mechatronic systems that play an essential role in the production of integrated circuits (IC) (Butler, 2011). The IC production requires the extremely accurate positioning of a substrate carrier, called the wafer stage, with respect to a light source. High-performance robust control is of crucial importance to meet the ever-increasing performance specifications.
In this section, model set identification for high-performance control for the prototype wafer stage depicted in Figure 6.4 is considered.
--[p180]-- The stage is closed-loop controlled in its six rigid body motion degrees of freedom, i.e., three translations and three rotations, using a PID-type non-optimal experimental controller $C ^ { \mathrm { e x p } }$ The controller achieves a bandwidth of $f _ { \mathrm { B W } } = 1 2 5 \mathrm { { H z } }$ in the horizontal directions, and 50Hz in the vertical directions.
The sampling frequency is $f _ { s } = 5 \mathrm { k H z }$ In this section, the identification of a single horizontal direction is considered.

===== [p19-19] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.4 Challenges in Identification of Future Semiconductor Equipment (2块) =====
--[p19]-- In this section, the state-of-art in system identification in semiconductor manufacturing is discussed, and the main research challenges are formulated.
--[p19]-- 1.4 Challenges in Identification of Future Semiconductor Equipment
The performance of future precision systems relies on advanced control strategies that can cope with the increased system complexity, as outlined in Section 1.3. In turn, the success of these control methods hinges on the availability of high-quality dynamic models. System identification provides the essential step towards obtaining such models from data.

===== [p219-219] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.7 Experimental validation: Robust inferential control (1块) =====
--[p219]-- 7.7 Experimental validation: Robust inferential control
In this section, the robust inferential control framework developed in Section 7.5 is experimentally validated on the wafer stage setup described in Section 7.2.1. The goals of the experimental study are 1) to demonstrate the relevance of RICR model sets in Section 7.5, and 2) to compare the performance of the inferential controller to a classical controller.

===== [p89-89] A Fast Smoothing-based Algorithm for Multivariable Experiment Design under $l _ { \infty } \mathbf { - n o r m }$ Signal Constraints<sup>1</sup> > 3.6.1 Active Vibration Isolation System (2块) =====
--[p89]-- 3.6.1 Active Vibration Isolation System
The experimental setup is the Active Vibration Isolation System (AVIS) shown in Figure 3.6. It is equipped with six voice-coil or Lorentz actuators, located at the four corners. Furthermore, it is equipped with six geophones that measure the absolute velocity of the system with respect to the floor. The system its six rigid-body motion degrees of freedom are controlled in closed-loop using a PID controller that achieves a bandwidth of 20Hz.
--[p89]-- Due to electro-mechanical limitations, peak amplitude constraints are imposed onto all six input and six output signals. Appropriate scaling is applied such that all constraint values are equal to one, $c _ { p } = 1 , \forall p$

===== [p203-203] Point of Interest Positioning in High-precision Motion Systems: a Robust Inferential Control Framework<sup>1</sup> > 7.3 Control performance analysis framework (1块) =====
--[p203]-- 7.3 Control performance analysis framework
In this section, a framework for the analysis of the best achievable control performance for non-collocated systems is presented. Herein, the implications of the non-collocation of the inputs and outputs in (7.1) for the controller structure and its dynamics are explicitly addressed. In addition, over-actuation and oversensing strategies are explored to achieve performance beyond the conventional performance limits.

===== [p16-16] Wafer Stage Motion Control from Experiment Design to Robust Performance > 1.3 Challenges in Control of Future Semiconductor Equipment (1块) =====
--[p16]-- 1.3 Challenges in Control of Future Semiconductor Equipment
The envisaged control strategies for future precision equipment necessitate the employment of advanced modeling and control techniques that can efectively cope with the increased system complexity. In this section, the current state-ofart in control of semiconductor equipment is discussed, and the key challenges for control of future systems are outlined.
